3I(f)+2&\mbox{if $\Sigma$ is non-orientable.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
3.4
) since
e\geq 1

and
g(\Sigma)\geq 0
if
\Sigma
is orientable (resp.
g(\widetilde{\Sigma})\geq 0

if
\Sigma
is non-orientable).
∎

Recall that we have fixed
\tau\in(0,\pi/10]
.
Let
{\alpha}_{1}={\alpha}_{1}(\tau)\in(0,\tau]
be the constant given by
Lemma
4.1
for
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
; observe that
the total length
L^{f}({r})
of the intersection of
f(\Sigma)

with a sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}({r})
of sufficiently large radius
{r}

is less than
L_{0}{r}
; this follows since for
{r}
large,

\frac{L^{f}({r})}{r}\sim 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{e}d_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\footnotesize{(Claim \ref{eqts})}}}}{{\leq}}\pi[3I(f)+3]\leq\pi[3(I_{0}+1)+3].

(5.11)

We can also pick a smallest
R>0
(only depending on
\tau
)
so that the following properties hold
(compare with properties (H0)-…-(H5) above):

(H0)’

The index of
f(\Sigma)\cap\mathbb{B}(R/3)
is
I(f)
.

(H1)’

f(\Sigma)\setminus\mathbb{B}(R/3)
consists of

e
multi-graphs over their
projections to planes
\Pi_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
passing though

\vec{0}
,
j=1,\ldots,e
.

(H2)’

The image through the Gauss map of
f
of
each component
C_{j}
of
f(\Sigma)\setminus\mathbb{B}(R/3)
is contained
in the spherical
neighborhood of radius
{\alpha}_{1}/2
centered at a point
v_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}(1)

perpendicular to
\Pi_{j}
(thus,
C_{j}
satisfies item (B2) of
Lemma
4.1
with
R_{1}=R/3
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}/2
).

(H3)’

f(\Sigma)
makes an angle greater than

\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{{\alpha}_{1}}{2}

with every sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}({r})
of radius
{r}\geq R/3
centered
at the origin (so,
C_{j}
satisfies item (B1)
of Lemma
4.1
with
R_{1}=R/3
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}/2
).

(H4)’

The total length of the intersection of
f(\Sigma)
with any sphere

\mathbb{S}^{2}({r})
centered at the origin and radius
{r}\geq R/3
is less than

(L_{0}-\frac{1}{2}){r}
(hence
C_{j}
satisfies item (B3)
of Lemma
4.1
with
R_{1}=R/3
).

Applying the last sentence in item 2 of
Proposition
8.4
with
I=I_{0}+1
and
B=0
, we deduce that:

(H5)’

The intrinsic distance in the pullback metric by
f

from
\vec{0}\in\Sigma
to any point in the boundary of

f^{-1}(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(R/2))
is at most
a(I_{0})R
, where

a(I_{0})=\frac{\widehat{C}(I_{0}+1,0)}{2}=\sqrt{6}(3I_{0}+1)\sqrt{I_{0}+2}+\frac{\pi}{4}(6I_{0}+11).

(5.12)

Given
r\in[\frac{R}{2},4R]
, let
{\Delta}_{n}(r)
be the
domain inside
M_{n}
given by Definition
5.2
, related
to the
f,R
above. Properties (H0)’-…-(H5)’ imply
that for
{\lambda}_{n}
large, the immersion
{\lambda}_{n}F_{n}
satisfies
(compare with properties (I0)-…-(I5) above):

(I0)’

The index of
({\lambda}_{n}F_{n})|_{\Delta_{n}(R/2)}
equals

I(f)
.

(I1)’

({\lambda}_{n}F_{n})(\Delta_{n}(4R)\setminus\Delta_{n}(R/2))
can
be considered to be a union of
e

multi-graphs over their projections to the
\Pi_{j}
,

j=1,\ldots,e
.
We denote these multi-graphs by
\widetilde{G}_{n}(1),\ldots,\widetilde{G}_{n}(e)
.

(I2)’

For
j=1,\ldots,e
, the image of
\widetilde{G}_{n}(j)
through the
“Gauss map” of

{\lambda}_{n}F_{n}
(defined through ambient parallel translation, see
Remark
4.2
) is contained in the spherical
neighborhood of radius
{\alpha}_{1}
centered at
v_{j}

(here we have identified
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the tangent space to

{\lambda}_{n}X
at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
).

(I3)’

\widetilde{G}_{n}(j)
makes an angle greater than
\frac{\pi}{2}-{\alpha}_{1}
with every geodesic sphere
\widetilde{S}({r})
in
{\lambda}_{n}X_{n}

centered at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
of radius
{r}\in[R/2,4R]
.

(I4)’

\mbox{\rm Length}[\widetilde{G}_{n}(j)\cap\widetilde{S}(R/2)]<L_{0}R/2
.

(I5)’

The intrinsic distance in the pullback metric by

{\lambda}_{n}F_{n}
on
M_{n}
, from
p_{1}(n)
to any point of the boundary of

\Delta_{n}(R/2)
is at most
[a(I_{0})+1]R
.

Back in the original scale, we have that
\Delta_{n}({r})\subset F_{n}^{-1}(\overline{B}_{X_{n}}(F_{n}(p_{1}(n)),{r}/{\lambda}_{n}))
for all
{r}\in[\frac{R}{2},4R]

and the following properties hold for
n
sufficiently large:

(J0)’

The index of
F_{n}|_{\Delta_{n}(R/2)}
equals
I(f)
.

(J1)’

F_{n}(\Delta_{n}(4R)\setminus\Delta_{n}(R/2))
is a union
of
e
multi-graphs over their projections
to the
\Pi_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,e
.
We call
G_{n}(1),\ldots,G_{n}(e)
to these multi-graphs.

(J2)’

For
j=1,\ldots,e
, the image of
G_{n}(j)
through the
“Gauss map” of
F_{n}
is contained in the spherical neighborhood of
radius
{\alpha}_{1}
centered at
v_{j}
.

(J3)’

G_{n}(j)
makes an angle greater than
\frac{\pi}{2}-{\alpha}_{1}

with every geodesic sphere
S({r})
in

X_{n}
centered at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
of radius

{r}\in[\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}},\frac{4R}{{\lambda}_{n}}]
.

(J4)’

\mbox{\rm Length}[G_{n}(j)\cap S(\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}})]<L_{0}\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}}
.

(J5)’

The intrinsic distance in the pullback metric by
F_{n}
on
M_{n}
,
from
p_{1}(n)
to any point of the boundary of
\Delta_{n}(R/2)
is at most

\frac{R}{{\lambda}_{n}}[a(I_{0})+1]
.

Therefore, given
{r}\in[\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}},\frac{2R}{{\lambda}_{n}}]
,
G_{n}(j)\cap[\overline{B}_{X_{n}}(F_{n}(p_{1}(n)),2{r})\setminus B_{X_{n}}(F_{n}(p_{1}(n)),{r})]

satisfies the hypotheses (B1), (B2), (B3) of Lemma
4.1

with the choices
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
, inner extrinsic radius
{r}
,
outer extrinsic radius
2{r}
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}
.

5.5.2
How to proceed if the (first) local pictures fail to have a
uniform size

Definition 5.8
.

Define
{r}_{n}
as the supremum of the extrinsic radii

{r}\geq 4R/{\lambda}_{n}
such that for all
j=1,\ldots,e
,
annular enlargements
\widehat{G}_{n}(j)
of the
G_{n}(j)

satisfy conditions (B1), (B2), (B3) of Lemma
4.1

for the choices
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
, inner extrinsic radius
R_{1}=\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}}
, outer extrinsic radius
R_{2}={r}_{n}
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}
.

Figure 3:
Schematic representation of the extrinsic geometry of the
immersion
(F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright X_{n})\in\Lambda=\Lambda(I_{0}+1,H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})

around a point
p_{1}(n)
where the maximum of
|A_{M_{n}}|
in
M_{n}
is
achieved. Here,
{\lambda}_{n}=|A_{M_{n}}|(p_{1}(n))
tends
to infinity and
{\lambda}_{n}F_{n}
converges as
n\to\infty

to the complete minimal immersion
f\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
with finite total
curvature. Horizontal distances in the figure represent
extrinsic distances in
X_{n}
measured from
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
.
For
n
large enough and
in the range of extrinsic radii between
\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{n}}

and
r_{n}\geq 4R/{\lambda}_{n}
,
F_{n}
consists of
e
multi-graphical pieces

\widehat{G}_{n}(1),\ldots,\widehat{G}_{n}(e)
, where
e
is the
number of ends of
f
.

Remark 5.9
.

1.

Unlike what happened in the case
I=1
(Section
5.4
),
we can no longer insure that
the outer extrinsic radius
r_{n}
is bounded from below
by some positive constant independent of
n

(i.e. Proposition
5.3
does not necessarily hold in our setting).
The reason for this difference is that in our current situation,
the estimate
I(f)\leq I_{0}+1

is not necessarily an equality (as it was when
I=1
), and thus, with the
notation in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, we cannot
insure that if
r_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
has index zero away from the origin for
n
large.

2.

If
r_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
has index zero away from the origin for

n
large in the sense that property
(\diamondsuit)
above
holds, then the arguments in the proof of Proposition
5.3
lead to a contradiction.
Hence we conclude that one of the two following excluding
possibilities holds:

(2.A)

\{r_{n}\}_{n}
is bounded away from zero, with this lower bound
being independent of the sequence
\{F_{n}\}_{n}\subset\Lambda
.
In this case, Proposition
5.3
holds (now

{\delta}_{3}\in(0,{\delta}_{2}]
is given by Definition
4.5
for the choices

L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1

and
m
being 1 plus the integer part of
\frac{1}{2}[3(I_{0}+1)+3]
, see
equation (
5.11
) which estimates the total spinning of
f
by
above; also see Proposition
5.16
below).
In this case, we can apply Proposition
5.17
below to
conclude the proof of items 1, 2, 3 of Theorem
1.2
.

(2.B)

There exists some sequence
\{F_{n}\}_{n}\subset\Lambda

(with associated base points
p_{1}(n)
) such that

r_{n}\to 0
and
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
fails to have index
zero away from the origin for
n
large, in the sense that
the property
(\diamondsuit)
above fails.

Assume that we are in case (2.B) above.
Roughly speaking, we will show that the immersions
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}

converge as
n\to\infty
to a possibly finitely branched,
complete minimal immersion
f_{2}\colon\Sigma_{2}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

away from finitely many points where curvature blows-up.
Furthermore,
\Sigma_{2}
is finitely connected and its Morse index is
at most
(I_{0}+1)-I(f_{1})\leq I_{0}
.
This compactness result is delicate and we will divide its proof into the
following two steps.

(M1)

Describe the behavior of the immersions
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}

near the origin as
n\to\infty
. We will
do this in Lemmas
5.10
and
5.11
.

(M2)

Analyze the
global
convergence of the
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}

(after passing to a subsequence) to a complete, finitely branched minimal
immersion
f_{2}\colon\Sigma_{2}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
with finite total curvature. We will
do this in Proposition
5.13
.

The proof of the next lemma follows easily from the behavior of the blow-down limit
of any of the
e
ends of the complete minimal immersion

f=f_{1}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined just after items (L1)-(L3).

Lemma 5.10
.

Relabel as
e_{1}=e
the number of ends of
f_{1}
. Suppose

r_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then, after choosing a subsequence,
each of the finite number of extended and scaled multi-graphs

(\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n})|_{\widehat{G}_{n}(j)}
, considered to be a
mapping on an open annulus, converges as
n\to\infty
to a
conformal minimal immersion of a punctured disk

f_{2,j}\colon\mathbb{D}^{*}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|z|<1\}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

(here
j\in\{1,\ldots,e_{1}\}
refers to the j-th end of
f_{1}
) with

f_{2,j}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\subset\mathbb{B}(1)\setminus\{\vec{0}\}
. Furthermore, for each such
j
,

1.

f_{2,j}
extends analytically to a possibly branched minimal disk

\overline{f}_{2,j}\colon\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}^{*}\cup\{0\}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

with
\overline{f}_{2,j}(0)=\vec{0}
;

2.

The branching order of
\overline{f}_{2,j}
at
0
is one less than the
multiplicity of the associated sequence of multi-graphs

(\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n})|_{\widehat{G}_{n}(j)}
. Such
multiplicity (which is independent of
n
large) coincides with the
spinning of associated j-th end of
f_{1}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{e_{1}}\}
be the set of
parameter domains of the associated branched minimal
disks
\{\overline{f}_{2,1},\ldots,\overline{f}_{2,e_{1}}\}
given by item 1
of Lemma
5.10
,
and consider the map
F_{\infty}\colon\cup\mathcal{D}\looparrowright\mathbb{B}(1)
be defined by

F_{\infty}|_{D_{i}}=\overline{f}_{2,i},\quad i=1,\ldots,e_{1}.

Observe that
\cup\mathcal{D}
(disjoint union) can be considered to be a
smooth surface.
Let
\mathcal{S}(0)\subset\cup\mathcal{D}
be the finite set of centers of the disks

D_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,e_{1}
. Consider the quotient space
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
of

\cup\mathcal{D}
where each of the elements in
\mathcal{S}(0)

identifies to one point that we denote by
\widehat{0}\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
,
and every other point of
\cup\mathcal{D}
only identifies with itself. Let

\pi\colon\cup\mathcal{D}\to\widehat{\mathcal{D}}

be the related quotient map, that is,
\pi|_{\mathcal{S}(0)}
is the
constant map equal to
\widehat{0}
,
and the restriction of
\pi
to
(\cup\mathcal{D})\setminus\mathcal{S}(0)

is injective. After endowing
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
with the quotient topology,

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
is a path-connected topological space and

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(0):=\pi(\mathcal{S}(0))=\{\widehat{0}\}.

(5.13)

Furthermore,
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(0)
is a smooth immersed
surface. In what follows, we will at times consider
the induced well-defined continuous map

F_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\looparrowright\mathbb{B}(1)
, which we denote the same way.

The next statement can be viewed as an direct consequence of
Lemma
5.10
.

Lemma 5.11
.

In the above situation, the following properties hold:

1.

F_{\infty}
restricted to
F_{\infty}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(1)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\frac{1}{2}))
consists of
e_{1}
multi-graphs.

2.

The sequence of immersions
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
restricted to the
component
\Delta_{2,n}\subset M_{n}
of

{\textstyle(\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n})^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{r_{n}}X_{n}}(\vec{0},1/2))}

that contains
p_{1}(n)
, converges as

n\to\infty
to
F_{\infty}
, where we consider

F_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\looparrowright\mathbb{B}(1)
to be defined on the quotient space

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

3.

The convergence in item 2 is smooth away from
\mathcal{S}(0)
, or from

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(0)
when we consider
F_{\infty}
to be defined on
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

Lemma
5.11
describes the convergence of (a subsequence of) the

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
in a neighborhood of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(0)
, to a family

F_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\looparrowright\mathbb{B}(1)
of minimal disks branched
at the origin, and finishes step (M1) above.

Step (M2) needs two ingredients, which are Lemma
5.12

and Proposition
5.13
below. The first one relies on the
validity of Theorem
1.2
for
I=I_{0}

(by the induction hypothesis), while
in Proposition
5.13
we will construct the complete,
finitely branched minimal
immersion
f_{2}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
of finite total Gaussian curvature,
which is the limit of a subsequence
of the
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
as a consequence of Lemma
5.12
.

We remark that the surfaces
M_{n}
and the associated
points
p_{1}(n)
in the next theorem are not the same surfaces
and points that we have been using previously in this section
with this notation; so the reader should keep in mind
this abuse of notation when reading the next result.

Lemma 5.12
.

Consider a sequence
(\widetilde{F}_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright\widetilde{X}_{n})\in\Lambda(I_{0},H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})

such that the following properties hold.

(N1)

\{\max_{M_{n}}|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|\}_{n}
is not bounded from
above. In particular, after passing to a subsequence we can assume there
exists
p_{1}(n)\in M_{n}
such that

\max_{M_{n}}|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|=|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|(p_{1}(n))>\max\{n,A_{1}\}\quad\mbox{for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$,}

where
A_{1}\in[A_{0},\infty)
is given in the statement of
Theorem
1.2
for
I=I_{0}
(which can be applied by the
induction hypothesis); observe that the existence of
p_{1}(n)
is
guaranteed by Lemma
5.1
.

(N2)

In harmonic coordinates centered at
\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n))
, hence

\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n))=\vec{0}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the metrics on
\widetilde{X}_{n}

converge uniformly in the
C^{0}
norm to the flat metric
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and the (constant) mean curvatures of the
\widetilde{F}_{n}
converge
to zero as
n\to\infty
.

Let
\Delta_{1}(n)
be the component of

\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(\overline{B}_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n),r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1)))

described in item
1
of Theorem
1.2
and let

\Delta_{1}(n,\frac{2}{3})=\Delta_{1}(n)\cap\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(\overline{B}_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),\frac{2}{3}r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1)))
.
Then, after replacing by a subsequence, the following hold:

1.

\{r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a positive number

r\in[{\delta}_{1},\frac{{\delta}}{2}]
, where

{\delta}_{1},{\delta}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}/2]
are given by Theorem
1.2
.

2.

Let
b
be the number of boundary components of
\Delta_{1}(n)
, which
is independent of
n
. Then, the
b
multi-graphs

\widetilde{F}_{n}(\Delta_{1}(n))\cap[\overline{B}_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1))\setminus B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1))]

described in item
2
of Theorem
1.2

converge as
n\to\infty
to
b
minimal multi-graphs in

\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},r)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},r/2)
, each of which satisfies
the same estimate (
1.1
) as the multi-graphs in the sequence that
converge to it.

3.

There exists
J\in\mathbb{N}
,
J\leq I_{0}
,
{\varepsilon}_{1}\in(0,r)

and a finite set for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

\textstyle{Q(n)=\{q_{1}(n)=p_{1}(n),q_{2}(n),\ldots,q_{J}(n)\}\subset B_{M_{n}}\left(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r\right),}

such that

(a)

|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|(q_{i}(n))>\max\{n,A_{1}\}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,J

and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
(compare to item 1.d of
Theorem
1.2
).

(b)

Given
i,j\in 1,\ldots,J
with

i\neq j
, the intrinsic distance in
M_{n}
between
q_{i}(n)
and
q_{j}(n)

is at least
{\varepsilon}_{1}
(compare to item 1.c of Theorem
1.2
).

(c)

Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|\}_{n}
is uniformly bounded in

B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s})

(compare to item
3
of Theorem
1.2
).

(d)

There exist (not necessarily distinct) points
x_{1}=\vec{0},x_{2},\ldots,x_{J}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)
(this is the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
its flat metric) such that when viewed in harmonic coordinates
in
\widetilde{X}_{n}
centered at
p_{1}(n)
,
the points
\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n))
converge as
n\to\infty

to
x_{i}
, for each
i=1,\ldots,J
.

4.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
large and fixed, and for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
,
there exist
{\delta}_{i}(s),{\delta}_{i}(1,s),r_{i}(n,s)

with
0<{\delta}_{i}(1,s)\leq r_{i}(n,s)\leq{\delta}_{i}(s)/2<{\delta}_{i}(s)<\frac{2{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s}
,
such that the following hold.
Let
A_{i}(n,s)
be the component of

\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n)),r_{i}(n,s)))

that contains
q_{i}(n)
. Then, there exists
s_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
integer
s\geq s_{0}
, there exists
N(s)\in\mathbb{N}
so that for
n\geq N(s)
:

(a)

The positive numbers
r_{i}(n,s)
converge as
n\to\infty
to
some
r_{i}(s)\in[{\delta}_{i}(1,s),{\delta}_{i}(s)/2]
.

(b)

A_{i}(n,s)
is compact with smooth non-empty boundary and

\widetilde{F}_{n}(\partial A_{i}(n,s))\subset\partial B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n)),r_{i}(n,s))

(compare to item 1.b of
Theorem
1.2
).

(c)

The number
\widetilde{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
of boundary components of
A_{i}(n,s)

is independent of
n,s
and the restriction of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
to an annular
neighborhood of each boundary component of
A_{i}(n,s)
is a multi-graph
of positive integer multiplicity
m_{h,i}
independent of
n,s

(here
h\in\{1,\ldots,\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
), whose related graphing
function
u=u_{n,s}
satisfies inequality (
1.1
)
for
n,s
sufficiently large, where
x
expresses harmonic coordinates in

B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n)),\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{2})

(compare to item
2
of Theorem
1.2
);
the union of these annular neighborhoods
of
\partial A_{i}(n,s)
can be taken to
be
A_{i}(n,s)\setminus\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n)),\textstyle{\frac{r_{i}(n,s)}{2}}))
.

(d)

The
\widetilde{F}_{n}
restricted to
\Delta_{1}(n,\frac{2}{3})\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}A_{i}(n,s)
converge smoothly as
n\to\infty
to a minimal
immersion

F_{\infty,s}\colon M_{s}\looparrowright\textstyle{\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)}

of a compact surface
M_{s}
with boundary, and

F_{\infty,s}(M_{s})\cap[\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{1}{2}r)]
consists of the intersection of the limiting
multi-graphs appearing in item 2 with

\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{1}{2}r)
.

(e)

The boundary
\partial M_{s}
decomposes into
J+1
collections of curves

\{{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,{\alpha}_{b}\},\quad\{{\beta}_{1,i}(s),\ldots,{\beta}_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}(s)\}_{i=1,\ldots,J}

(recall that
b
is the number of boundary components of
\Delta_{1}(n)
)
where
F_{\infty,s}({\alpha}_{h})\subset\partial\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)
for each

h=1,\ldots,b
, and
F_{\infty,s}({\beta}_{l,i}(s))\subset\partial\mathbb{B}(x_{i},r_{i}(s))
for some
i=1,\ldots,J
and
for every
l=1,\ldots,\widetilde{e}_{i}
.

5.

There exists an infinite
strictly increasing sequence

\mathfrak{S}=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{j},\ldots\}\subset\mathbb{N}

such that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for
n
sufficiently large depending on
j
,

A_{i}(n,s_{j+1})\subset\mbox{\rm Int}(A_{i}(n,s_{j}))\;\mbox{\rm and }\;\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s_{j}))=\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s_{1})).

In particular, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
n
sufficiently large depending
on
j
,
A_{i}(n,s_{1})\setminus A_{i}(n,s_{j})
is stable.

6.

For each
s_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}
defined in item 5,

M_{s_{j+1}}\subset M_{s_{j}}
and

F_{\infty,s_{j+1}}|_{M_{s}}=F_{\infty,s_{j}}
.
Then
M_{\infty}=\cup_{s_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}M_{s_{j}}
is a compact
Riemann surface with boundary,
punctured in
e:=\sum_{i=1}^{J}\widetilde{e}_{i}
points

\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,J}
,
and the immersion
F_{\infty}\colon M_{\infty}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by

F_{\infty}|_{M_{s}}=F_{\infty,s}
extends to a finitely branched minimal
immersion

\overline{F}_{\infty}\colon M_{\infty}\cup\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,J}\looparrowright\textstyle{\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)},

such that
\overline{F}_{\infty}(\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\})=\{x_{i}\}
,

i=1,\ldots,J
, and the branch points of
\overline{F}_{\infty}
are contained
in the set
\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\ |\ i=1,\ldots,J\}
.

7.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
fixed and
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small
and fixed, the branching contribution
B_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
to

\overline{F}_{\infty}
from
\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}
is

B_{i}=S_{i}-\widetilde{e}_{i}
, where

S_{i}=\sum_{h=1}^{\widetilde{e}_{i}}m_{h,i}

(5.14)

is the total spinning of the boundary curves of
\widetilde{F}_{n}

restricted to the component

\Delta(i,n,{\varepsilon})
of
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n)),{\varepsilon}))

containing
q_{i}(n)
(for
n
sufficiently large,
S_{i}
is independent
of
n
). Furthermore,

S_{i}\leq 3I(\Delta(i,n,{\varepsilon}));

(5.15)

(here
I(\Delta(i,n,{\varepsilon}))
is the index
of
\Delta(i,n,{\varepsilon})
) and so, the total branching of

\overline{F}_{\infty}
is at most
\sum_{i=1}^{J}(S_{i}-\widetilde{e}_{i})\leq 3I_{0}-J
.

Proof.

Since
[{\delta}_{1},\frac{{\delta}}{2}]
is compact, after replacing by a
subsequence, the sequence
\{r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1)\}_{n}\subset[{\delta}_{1},\frac{{\delta}}{2}]

given by Theorem
1.2
converges to a positive number
r\in[{\delta}_{1},\frac{{\delta}}{2}]
. The convergence stated in item 2 of the
multi-graphs

\widetilde{F}_{n}(\Delta_{1}(n))\cap[\overline{B}_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1))\setminus B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}r_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}(1))]

to minimal multi-graphs in
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},r)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{1}{2}r)

is standard by curvature estimates for CMC graphs.
This gives items 1 and 2 of the lemma.

We next prove that item 3 holds. To find the finite set
Q(n)
, we will
proceed as follows. Suppose for the moment that, after
replacing by a subsequence, for each
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|
is
uniformly bounded in
B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3s}r)
.
In this case, the set
Q(n):=\{q_{1}(n)=p_{1}(n)\}
is easily
seen to satisfy item 3 of the lemma with the choice
{\varepsilon}_{1}=\frac{2}{3}r
.
Otherwise, after replacing by a subsequence, there exists an
s_{1}\in\mathbb{N}

and a point
q_{2}(n)\in B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3s_{1}}r)
such that
|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|(q_{2}(n))>\max\{n,10A_{1}\}
. If after replacing by a subsequence,

\{A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}\}_{n}
is uniformly bounded
in
B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r)\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{2}B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\frac{2}{3s}r)]

for each
s\in\mathbb{N}
, then the set
Q(n):=\{q_{1}(n),q_{2}(n)\}

satisfies item 3 of the lemma with

{\varepsilon}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}d_{M_{n}}(q_{1}(n),q_{2}(n))
,
since after replacing by another subsequence,
\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{2}(n))
converges
as
n\to\infty
to some
x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)

(note that
x_{2}
might be
\vec{0}
).
Continuing inductively, we arrive at two sets of points

Q(n)=\{q_{1}(n)=p_{1}(n),q_{2}(n),\ldots,q_{J}(n)\},\quad\{x_{1}=\vec{0},x_{2},\ldots,x_{J}\}\subset\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},{\textstyle\frac{2}{3}r}),

satisfying item 3 of the lemma with respect to

{\varepsilon}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\min\{d_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),q_{j}(n))\ |\ i,j=1,\ldots,J,\ i\neq j\}.

Here,
J\leq I_{0}
because the index of
B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{2}{3}r)

is at most
I_{0}
. This finishes the proof of item 3 of the lemma.

Regarding item 4, we make the following two observations:

(O1)

For each
s\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a uniform upper
bound
A_{2}(s)\geq A_{0}
on the norm of the
second fundamental forms of the immersions
\widetilde{F}_{n}
restricted to

\bigcup_{i=1}^{J}\left[\overline{B}_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s}})\setminus B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{4s}})\right].

(this follows from the already proven item 3(c) of this lemma).

(O2)

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\widehat{F}_{n,s}
be the restriction
of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
to

\bigcup_{i=1}^{J}\overline{B}_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s}})
.
Then, observation (O1) implies that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\widehat{F}_{n,s}

lies in the space
\Lambda(I_{0},H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{1}/12s,A_{2}(s),K_{0})
.

We next apply Theorem
1.2
to
\widehat{F}_{n,s}\in\Lambda(I_{0},H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{1}/12s,A_{2}(s),K_{0})

(which can be applied by the induction hypothesis), from where one has a
corresponding constant
\widehat{A}_{1}(s)\geq A_{1}
that replaces the previous
constant
A_{1}
and where the choice of
\tau
is the same as previously
considered. Assume that
n
is chosen sufficiently large,
so that given
i=1,\ldots,J
, the point
q_{i}(n)
satisfies that the
maximum of the norm of the second fundamental form of

\widehat{F}_{n,s}
in
\overline{B}_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s})
is achieved at

q_{i}(n)
, with value greater than
10\widehat{A}_{1}(s)

(by item
1
(d) of Theorem
1.2
). Another consequence
of Theorem
1.2
applied to
\widehat{F}_{n,s}
is that
for
n
large and for each
i=1,\ldots,J
, we have
associated positive numbers

{\delta}_{i}(s),{\delta}_{i}(1,s),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i,s)

with
{\delta}_{i}(1,s),{\delta}_{i}(s),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i,s)
) playing the respective
roles of the related numbers
{\delta}_{1},{\delta},r_{F}(i)
in
Theorem
1.2
, where

0<{\delta}_{i}(1,s)\leq r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i,s)\leq{\delta}_{i}(s)/2<{\delta}_{i}(s)<\frac{2{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s}\quad\mbox{for all $n$.}

(5.16)

We also have a
\Delta
-type domain

\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i))
defined by
item
1
of Theorem
1.2
, that is,
\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i))
is the component of

\widehat{F}_{n,s}^{-1}(\overline{B}_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n,s}(q_{i}(n)),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i)))
containing
q_{i}(n)
, so that
the conclusions of Theorem
1.2
hold for these

{\delta}_{i}(s),{\delta}_{i}(1,s),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i,s),\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i))
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i))

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s}}),

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{n,s}\left[\partial\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i))\right]

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\partial B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widehat{F}_{n,s}(q_{i}(n)),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i)).

(5.17)

We next check that the domains and numbers

A_{i}(n,s):=\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i)),\qquad r_{i}(n,s):=r_{\widehat{F}_{n,s}}(i)

satisfy items 4(a)-…-4(e) stated in the lemma.
Item 4(a) follows directly from (
5.16
), and item 4(b) follows
from (
5.17
).
Regarding item 4(c), since
A_{i}(n,s)
is compact,
its boundary has a finite number

\widetilde{e}_{i}(n,s)\in\mathbb{N}
of components. By item
2
of
Theorem
1.2
,
each boundary component of
A_{i}(n,s)
admits an annular neighborhood
which is a multi-graph of positive integer multiplicity

m_{h,i}(n,s)\in\mathbb{N}
(the index
h

parameterizes the set of boundary components of
A_{i}(n,s)
).
The fact that both the
number of such boundary components and the multiplicities

m_{h,i}(n,s)
can be considered to be independent of
n,s

(after passing to a subsequence in
n
)
follow from the fact the
m_{h,i}(n,s)
are bounded independently of
n
,
which in turn can be deduced from the following inequality
(see item
B
(a) of Theorem
1.2
):

m_{h,i}(n,s)\leq 3\,\mbox{Index}(A_{i}(n,s))\leq 3\,I_{0},\quad\mbox{for all $n$.}

Now, the rest of properties stated in item 4(c) of the lemma are direct consequences of
Theorem
1.2
applied to
\widehat{F}_{n,s}
.

The convergence statement in item 4(d) follows from standard curvature
estimates for CMC immersions. The last sentence in item 4(d) follows from
uniqueness of the limit as
n\to\infty
of the
\widetilde{F}_{n}
restricted to

\Delta_{1}(n,\frac{2}{3})\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}\Delta_{i}(q_{i}(n),r_{\widehat{F}_{n}}(i))

and from the already proven item 2 of this lemma. Item 4(e) holds by
construction, which finishes the proof of item 4 of the lemma.

Regarding item 5, choose
s_{1}^{\prime}=1
and
s_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{2}^{\prime}>1
,
such that
\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s_{2}^{\prime}}<{\delta}_{i}(1,1)
. Assuming

s_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{j}^{\prime}
defined, choose
s_{j+1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

s_{j+1}^{\prime}>s_{j}^{\prime}
and
\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{3s_{j+1}^{\prime}}<{\delta}_{i}(s_{j}^{\prime},1)
.
This inequality implies that

A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j+1})\subset\mbox{\rm Int}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j}))
and so,

\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j+1}))\leq\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j}))
.
Since
\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{1}))
is finite,
there exists
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j}))=\mbox{\rm Index}(A_{i}(n,s^{\prime}_{j_{0}}))
for all
j\geq j_{0}
.
Now label
s_{j}=s^{\prime}_{j+j_{0}}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and item 5 of the lemma
is proved.

By item 4(d), for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
the restrictions of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
to

\Delta_{1}(n,\frac{2}{3})\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}A_{i}(n,s_{j})

converge smoothly as
n\to\infty
to a minimal
immersion
F_{\infty,s_{j}}\colon M_{s_{j}}\looparrowright\textstyle{\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)}

of a compact surface
M_{s_{j}}
with boundary.
Since
A_{i}(n,s_{j+1})\subset\mbox{\rm Int}(A_{i}(n,s_{j}))

then
M_{s_{j+1}}\subset M_{s_{j}}
for each
j
,
and by uniqueness of the limit we have

F_{\infty,s_{j+1}}|_{M_{s}}=F_{\infty,s_{j}}
for each
j
.

By a standard diagonal argument in
n
and
s_{j}
,
the map

F_{\infty}\colon M_{\infty}=\bigcup_{s_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}M_{s_{j}}\looparrowright\textstyle{\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\textstyle{\frac{2}{3}r})},

given by
F_{\infty}|_{M_{s_{j}}}=F_{\infty,s_{j}}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
is a minimal immersion with finite area,
defined on a surface
M_{\infty}
of finite genus: the bound on the
genus of
M_{\infty}
is the same bound as on the genus of the surface

\Delta_{1}(n)
, which, by item
B
of Theorem
1.2
,
is at most
6I(\Delta_{1}(n))-8\leq 6I_{0}-8

if the index
I(\Delta_{1}(n))\geq 2
; if
I(\Delta_{1}(n))=1
,
then the genus of
\Delta_{1}(n)
is zero.
Observe that
M_{\infty}
has at least
J

annular ends, and the number
e
of these ends of
M_{\infty}

is finite (at most
3I_{0}-1
by item B of Theorem
1.2
).
Furthermore, the image by
\widehat{F}_{\infty}
of these ends of
M_{\infty}

is
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J}\}\subset\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)
.
By regularity results in
[
10
]
,
M_{\infty}
is a compact
Riemann surface with
b
boundary components, punctured in

e:=\sum_{i=1}^{J}\widetilde{e}_{i}
points,
and we can denote the set of ends of
M_{\infty}
by

\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,J},

in such a way that the immersion
F_{\infty}
extends to a finitely
branched minimal immersion

\overline{F}_{\infty}\colon M\cup\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,J}\looparrowright\textstyle{\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\frac{2}{3}r)},

(5.18)

such that
\overline{F}_{\infty}(\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\})=\{x_{i}\}
,

i=1,\ldots,J
, and by construction, the set of branch points
of
\overline{F}_{\infty}
is contained in the set

\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\ |\ i=1,\ldots,J\}
.
This proves item 6 of the lemma.

Finally, we prove item 7. Observe that the branching order

B(P_{h,i})\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
of

\overline{F}_{\infty}
at
P_{h,i}
equals

B(P_{h,i})=m_{h,i}-1,

(5.19)

where
m_{h,i}\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity defined in item 4(c) above.
By adding this in the
set
\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{\widetilde{e}_{i},i}\}
,
we deduce that the branching contribution

B_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
to
\overline{F}_{\infty}
from this set is

B_{i}=S_{i}-\widetilde{e}_{i}
, where
S_{i}=\sum_{h=1}^{\widetilde{e}_{i}}m_{h,i}
,
and thus, (
5.14
) is proved. Finally,
the estimate (
5.15
) for the total spinning of
\Delta(i,n,{\varepsilon})

(for a sufficiently small
{\varepsilon}>0
) follows from
item B of Theorem
1.2
.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now come back to item (M2) above.
Using the notation in Lemma
5.11
,
suppose, after choosing a subsequence,
that the
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
restricted to

\Delta_{2,n}
converge to a family

F_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\looparrowright\mathbb{B}(1)

(5.20)

of minimal disks branched at the origin as described in
Lemmas
5.10
and
5.11
.
Thus, the desired (global) limit
f_{2}\colon\Sigma_{2}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
is already
constructed in a neighborhood of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(0)
in
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}

(see equation (
5.13
)),
where a non-trivial part of the index of
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
is collapsing
(namely, this collapsing index is
I(f_{1})>0
);
since the remaining index of

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
is at most
(I_{0}+1)-I(f_{1})\leq I_{0}
, we are
allowed to apply Lemma
5.12
to
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
. We next
make this paragraph and the previously alluded to global convergence
in item (M2) rigorous.

Proposition 5.13
.

In the situation above, let
\widetilde{F}_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright\widetilde{X}_{n}
be

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright\frac{1}{r_{n}}X_{n}
. Then, after replacing by a subsequence, there exist

R_{0}\geq 10,{\varepsilon}_{2}\in(0,{\delta}_{1}]
and a
collection of points
Q_{2}(n)=\{q_{1}(n)=p_{1}(n),q_{2}(n),\ldots,q_{J}(n)\}\subset B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R_{0})
,
J\leq I_{0}
, such that:

1.

For any
R>R_{0}
,
\{|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|\}_{n}
is
uniformly bounded in

B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R)\setminus B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R_{0})
.

2.

d_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),q_{j}(n))\geq{\varepsilon}_{2}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

i\neq j\in\{1,2,\ldots,J\}
.

3.

For each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,J\}
and

m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\frac{1}{m}<{\varepsilon}_{2}
,

|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|(q_{i}(n))>n=\max\{|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|(x):x\in B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),1/m)\}

and there exists
A_{2}(m)>1
such that
|A_{\widetilde{F}_{n}}|<A_{2}(m)
in

B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R_{0})\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),1/m)
.

4.

There exist (not necessarily distinct) points
x_{1}=\vec{0},x_{2},\ldots,x_{J}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},R_{0})
(here
\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},R)
denotes
the ball centered the origin with radius
R>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with its flat
metric) such that when viewed in harmonic coordinates in
\widetilde{X}_{n}

centered at
\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n))
, the points
\widetilde{F}_{n}(q_{i}(n))
converge as

n\to\infty
to
x_{i}
, for each
i=1,2,\ldots,J
.

5.

For almost all
R>R_{0}
and for
m
sufficiently large, the

\widetilde{F}_{n}
restricted to

{\textstyle\overline{B}_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R)\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{J}B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\frac{1}{m})}

converge smoothly as
n\to\infty
to a minimal immersion

F_{\infty,m,R}\colon M_{m,R}\looparrowright\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},R)

of a compact surface with boundary
M_{m,R}
. Furthermore,

M_{m,R}\subset M_{m+1,R^{\prime}}
and

F_{\infty,m+1,R^{\prime}}|_{M_{m,R}}=F_{\infty,m,R}

whenever
R^{\prime}>R>R_{0}
.

6.

Define

\Sigma_{2}^{*}:=\bigcup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\in\mathbb{N}}}{{R>R_{0}}}}M_{m,R},\qquad f_{2}^{*}\colon\Sigma_{2}^{*}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3},\ f_{2}^{*}|_{M_{m,R}}=F_{\infty,m,R}.

Then,
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
is a (possibly disconnected) open Riemann surface and

f_{2}^{*}
is a minimal immersion. Furthermore, the conformal completion

\overline{\Sigma}_{2}
of
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
has the structure of a compact Riemann surface,

\overline{\Sigma}_{2}\setminus\Sigma_{2}^{*}=\mathcal{S}(f_{2})\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
is a finite set,
and
f_{2}^{*}\colon\Sigma_{2}^{*}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
extends through
\mathcal{S}(f_{2})

to a finitely branched, complete minimal immersion

f_{2}\colon\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}^{*}\cup\mathcal{S}(f_{2})\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

with finite total curvature, where

(a)

\mathcal{S}(f_{2})
is the disjoint union of the finite set

\mathcal{S}(\vec{0})=\{P_{1,1},\ldots P_{e_{1},1}\}\subset f_{2}^{-1}(\{x_{1}=\vec{0}\})

that appears in Lemma
5.11
, together with the closely related finite sets

\mathcal{S}(x_{i})=\{P_{1,i},\ldots,P_{b_{i},i}\}\subset f_{2}^{-1}(\{x_{i}\}),\quad i=2,\ldots J.

Furthermore, the set of branch points
of
f_{2}
is contained in
\mathcal{S}(f_{2})

and its branch locus (image) is contained
in
\{x_{1}=\vec{0},x_{2},\ldots,x_{J}\}\subset\mathbb{B}(R_{0})
.

(b)

The set of ends of
f_{2}
is
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{e_{2}}\}
.

(c)

The map
F_{\infty}
given in (
5.20
) coincides with
f_{2}
in a
neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}(x_{1}=\vec{0})
in
\Sigma_{2}
.

7.

The total
branching order
B(f_{2})
of
f_{2}
can be estimated from above as follows:

B(f_{2})\leq 3[I_{0}+1-\mbox{\rm Index}(f_{1})]-J\leq 3I_{0}-1.

(5.21)

8.

The following properties hold for some
R>3R_{0}
:

(a)

The index of
f_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(R/3))
is
I(f_{2})
(compare to property
(H0)’ above).

(b)

f_{2}(\Sigma_{2})\setminus\mathbb{B}(R/3)
consists of
e_{2}
multi-graphs
over their projections to planes
\Pi_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
passing though

\vec{0}
,
j=1,\ldots,e_{2}
(compare to property (H1)’).
Furthermore, each of these end representatives contains no non-trivial
geodesic arcs with boundary points in the boundary
of
\Sigma_{2}\setminus f_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(R/3))
.

(c)

The image through the Gauss map of
f_{2}
of each component
C_{j}
of

f_{2}(\Sigma_{2})\setminus\mathbb{B}(R/3)
is contained in the spherical
neighborhood of radius
{\alpha}_{1}/2
centered at a point
v_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}(1)

perpendicular to
\Pi_{j}
, where
{\alpha}_{1}={\alpha}_{1}(\tau)\in(0,\tau]
is the
constant given by Lemma
4.1
for
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1

(thus,
C_{j}
satisfies item (B2) of Lemma
4.1

with
R_{1}=R/3
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}/2
, compare to item (H2)’).

(d)

f_{2}(\Sigma_{2})
makes an angle greater than

\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{{\alpha}_{1}}{2}

with every sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}({r})
of radius
r\geq R/3
centered at the
origin (so,
C_{j}
satisfies item (B1) of Lemma
4.1
with

R_{1}=R/3
and
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}/2
, compare
to (H3)’).

(e)

The total length of the intersection of
f_{2}(\Sigma_{2})
with
any sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}({r})
centered at the origin and radius
r\geq R/3

is less than
(L_{0}-\frac{1}{2})r
(hence
C_{j}
satisfies item (B3) of
Lemma
4.1
with
R_{1}=R/3
,
compare to (H4)’).

(f)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the component

\Delta_{2,n}(R/3)
of
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(p_{1}(n)),R/3))
that
contains
p_{1}(n)
has index at least
I(f_{1})+I(f_{2})+(J-1)
and
if
J=1
, then
I(f_{2})>0
. In particular,

I(\Delta_{2,n}(R/3))>I(f_{1})
.

Proof.

Recall the notation and statement of Lemma
5.11
.
By assumption, the
\widetilde{F}_{n}
restricted to
\Delta_{2,n}
converge to

F_{\infty}
given by equation (
5.20
). Since the restriction of

F_{\infty}
to
F_{\infty}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(1)\setminus\mathbb{B}(\frac{1}{2}))
consists
of
e_{1}
multi-graphs (here
e_{1}
is the number of ends of
f_{1}
),
then
\widetilde{F}_{n}(\Delta_{2,n})
is graphical in the region

B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}(p_{1}(n)),1)\setminus B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}(p_{1}(n)),\frac{1}{2})
, and thus, the surfaces

{\textstyle M^{\prime}_{n}=M_{n}\setminus[\Delta_{2,n}\cap\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\widetilde{F}(p_{1}(n)),\frac{1}{2})]}

have uniform curvature estimates in a fixed sized
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{0}
-neighborhood
of its boundary (for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}]
).
Let
F^{\prime}_{n}\colon M_{n}^{\prime}\looparrowright\widetilde{X}_{n}
be the restriction of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
to

M_{n}^{\prime}
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can consider
F_{n}^{\prime}
to be an element
in a fixed related space
\Lambda^{\prime}
except that
the index of the immersions in

\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda(I_{0},H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime},A_{0},K_{0})

is at most
I_{0}
.
By induction, we can suppose that Theorem
1.2
holds for
the subspace
\Lambda^{\prime}
.

The construction of the finite set
\{q_{2}(n),\ldots,q_{J}(n)\}\subset B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),R_{0})
,
J\leq I_{0}
, appearing in the statement of
the proposition, follows exactly the same arguments used to prove the
existence of the related set
Q(n)
given in item 3 of
Lemma
5.12
.
Similarly, items 2, 3 and 4 of the proposition can be deduced
from the same reasoning as items 3(b), 3(c) and 3(d) of
Lemma
5.12
respectively; in particular, we use the
number
{\delta}_{1}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}/2]
defined in item 1 of Lemma
5.12

in order to find
{\varepsilon}_{2}\in(0,{\delta}_{1}]
satisfying item 2 of the
proposition. We leave the details to the reader.

The existence of the number
R_{0}\geq 10
and item 1 of
the proposition follows from the fact that the number
J
of sequences

\{q_{1}(n)=p_{1}(n)\}_{n},\ldots,\{q_{J}(n)\}_{n}

around which the second fundamental form of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
fails to be
bounded, is finite (at most
I_{0}+1
, by Lemma
5.1
).

Items 5 and 6 of the proposition also follow with small modifications from
the proof of items 4(d) and 6 of Lemma
5.12
,
where one also uses the fact that a complete minimal surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

with compact boundary and finite index has finite total curvature
(see
[
9
]
for this result when the surface is orientable
and see the last paragraph of the proof of
Theorem 17 in Ros
[
24
]
for the non-orientable case).
The proof of item 7 of the proposition
follows from the same arguments that proved item 7 of
Lemma
5.12
,
observe that the index of
f_{2}
is at most
(I_{0}+1)-\mbox{Index}(f_{1})
.

The proof of items 8(a) and of the first statement of 8(b) are clear
after taking
R>0
sufficiently large, since
f_{2}
has finite total
curvature. The second statement of 8(b) follows from the fact that
for
R>0
sufficiently large, the collection of ends

f_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\mathbb{B}(R/3))
of
f_{2}
is
foliated by the simple closed curves
in
\{f_{2}^{-1}(\partial\mathbb{B}(R^{\prime}))\mid R^{\prime}\geq R/3)\}
, each of which has
positive geodesic curvature.
The proof of parts (c) – (e) of item 8 also follow from previous
considerations (compare to items (H2)’–(H4)’).

To finish the proof of the proposition, we check that item 8(f) holds.
First suppose that
J=1
. In this case, the sequence

\{\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}\}_{n}

converges smoothly (up to a subsequence) to
f_{2}
in a neighborhood
of
\partial\mathbb{B}(1)
. This implies, by construction of
r_{n}

(see Definition
5.8
), that
f_{2}
is not flat in any neighborhood
of
\partial\mathbb{B}(1)
. In particular,
f_{2}
is not flat and the
image of its branch locus is the origin. Then, by item 1 of
Lemma
3.4
,
f_{2}
has positive index.

Regardless of the value of
J
, and
by the already proven item 8(a) of this proposition,
the index of
f_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(R/3))
is
I(f_{2})
.
Since the index of a compact minimal surface with boundary
remains the same after removing a sufficiently small neighborhood of
a finite subset of its interior, we deduce that for

m
sufficiently large, the index of

f_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(R/3))\setminus[\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}})\cup(\cup_{i=2}^{J}\mathbb{B}(x_{i},\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))]

(5.22)

is also equal to
I(f_{2})
. Let
\Delta_{2,n}(R/3)
be the component
of
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{-1}(B_{\widetilde{X}_{n}}(\vec{0},R/3))
that contains
p_{1}(n)
.
By the convergence in item 5 of the proposition,
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,
the index of

\Delta_{2,n}^{*}(R/3):=\Delta_{2,n}(R/3)\setminus[B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}})\cup(\cup_{i=2}^{J}B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))]

is equal to the index of the surface in (
5.22
).
Observe that for
n
sufficiently large and
m
large and fixed,
that index of
B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\frac{1}{m})
is equal to the
index
I(f_{1})
of
f_{1}
, and each of the balls in the pairwise disjoint
collection

\{B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}),B_{M_{n}}(q_{2}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}})),\ldots,B_{M_{n}}(q_{J}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))\}

is unstable. Then, if we denote by
I(S)
the Morse index of
a surface
S
, we get (after replacing by a subsequence)

\displaystyle I(\Delta_{2,n}(R/3))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle I(\Delta_{2,n}^{*}(R/3))+I(B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))+\displaystyle{\sum_{i=2}^{J}}I(B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))

(5.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(f_{2})+I(f_{1})+\displaystyle{\sum_{i=2}^{J}}I(B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}})).

If
J=1
, then the last sum is empty and (
5.23
) gives

I(\Delta_{2,n}(R/3))\geq I(f_{2})+I(f_{1})>I(f_{1})
as desired. Finally,
if
J\geq 2
, then we estimate each

I(B_{M_{n}}(q_{i}(n),\textstyle{\frac{1}{m}}))\geq 1
and so,
(
5.23
) gives
I(\Delta_{2,n}(R/3))\geq I(f_{2})+I(f_{1})+(J-1)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

With the notation of Proposition
5.13
consider the partition of

\mathcal{S}(f_{2})\subset\Sigma_{2}
by the subsets

\mathcal{S}(f_{2},i)=\mathcal{S}(x_{i}),\ i=1,\ldots,J

introduced in item 6(a) of that proposition.
Define the quotient space
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
of
\Sigma_{2}
where each of the
elements in
\mathcal{S}(f_{2},i)
identifies to one point that we denote by

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2},i)\in\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
,
i=1,\ldots,J

and every other point of
\Sigma_{2}
only identifies with itself. Let

\pi\colon\Sigma_{2}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}

be the related quotient map, that is,
\pi|_{\mathcal{S}(f_{2},i)}
is the
constant map equal to
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2},i)
,
and the restriction of
\pi
to
\Sigma_{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}(f_{2})

is injective. After endowing
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
with the quotient topology,
the following hold:

1.

\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
is a path-connected topological space and

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2}):=\pi(\mathcal{S}(f_{2}))

consists of
J
elements in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
.

2.

\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
is a smooth Riemannian surface
that induces a metric space structure
d_{\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}}
on
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
.

3.

The restriction of
f_{2}
to
\Sigma_{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}(f_{2})
,
considered to be a subset of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
,
extends to a continuous mapping
\widehat{f}_{2}\colon\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

4.

Let
p=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2},1)
(so
\widehat{f}_{2}(p)=\vec{0}
). Given
q\in\widehat{f}_{2}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}(R))
a point different from
p
, where
R>0
was defined
in item 8 of Proposition
5.13
,
there is an injective continuous path

{\alpha}_{p,q}\colon[0,1]\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
of least length joining
p
to
q

satisfying:

(a)

\widehat{f}_{2}\circ{\alpha}_{p,q}
is a piecewise smooth curve in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

with image in the ball
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(R)
.

(b)

{\alpha}_{p,q}([0,1])\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
consists of
j_{1}(q)\leq J

smooth geodesic arcs in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
,
each of which has length less than
\widehat{C}R
,
where
\widehat{C}=\widehat{C}(I_{0},B)>0
is defined in item 2 of
Proposition
8.4
and
B
is the total branching order
of
f_{2}
(recall that
B\leq 3I_{0}-1
by (
5.21
)).

(c)

In particular, as
j_{1}(q)\leq J\leq I_{0}
, then (compare to
item (H5)’ above)

d_{\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}}(p,q)<I_{0}\widehat{C}R.

(5.24)

Proof.

The path-connectedness of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
follows immediately
from the fact that for all
R>R_{0}
(this
R_{0}
is defined
in Proposition
5.13
),

B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n)),R)
is path-connected with
\mathcal{S}(f_{2})\subset B_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n)),R_{0})
and because the projection of a continuous path
in
\Sigma_{2}
to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
is a continuous path.
This proves that item 1 holds;
the proof of items 2 and 3 follow from the definition of the quotient
space
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}
and the fact that the
composition of continuous mappings is continuous.

The existence of the embedded minimizing geodesic
{\alpha}_{p,q}
joining
p

to
q
is standard, where
{\alpha}_{p,q}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
consists of a finite number
j_{1}(q)\leq J
of open
geodesic arcs that have least-length joining their endpoints;
the reason that there are at most
J
such arcs in
{\alpha}_{p,q}

follows from the fact that if there is more
than one such geodesic arc in
{\alpha}_{p,q}
, then each such arc contains a
point of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})\setminus\{p\}
.
Clearly
f_{2}\circ{\alpha}_{p,q}
is a piecewise smooth curve in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

and its image is contained in
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(R)
by the second statement in
item 8(b) of Proposition
5.13
, which completes
the proof of item 4(a).

Since
{\alpha}_{p,q}
is injective, length-minimizing
and only fails to be smooth at points in
\widehat{S}(f_{2})
, then

{\alpha}_{p,q}([0,1])\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
consists of
j_{1}(q)\leq J

smooth geodesic arcs in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(f_{2})
.
Item 4(b) follows directly from item 2 of
Proposition
8.4
(note that
I(f_{2})\leq I_{0}
by item 8(f) of
Proposition
5.13
since
I(f_{1})>0
)).
As
j_{1}(q)\leq J
and
J\leq I_{0}
by Proposition
5.13
,
then item 4(c) is proved.
∎

5.5.3
Finding an
s_{0}
-th local picture with a uniform size

Recall that in Definition
5.8
we introduced
r_{n}
in terms of

{\lambda}_{1,n}:={\lambda}_{n}
and a certain
R>0

given in terms of the limit immersion
f_{1}
so that hypotheses (B1),
(B2), (B3) of Lemma
4.1

hold for annular portions of the
F_{n}
with the choices

L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
. We now proceed
in a similar manner replacing
f_{1}
by
f_{2}
and
F_{n}
by

\widetilde{F}_{n}=\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}
. Properties 8(b)-8(e)
of Proposition
5.13
for
f_{2}
are similar to properties
(H1)’-(H4)’ for
f_{1}
. Recall that these
properties (H1)’-(H4)’ produce related properties (I1)’-(I4)’ for

{\lambda}_{n}F_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
large, in particular,
we found
e_{1}
multi-graphical annuli
\widetilde{G}_{n}(1),\ldots,\widetilde{G}_{n}(e_{1})

in
({\lambda}_{n}F_{n})(\Delta_{n}(4R)\setminus\Delta_{n}(R/2))
,
see property (I1)’. We now call
{\lambda}_{2,n}=\frac{1}{r_{n}}

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which tends
to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
by (2.B) in Remark
5.9
.
Reasoning analogously as we did with the first limit
f_{1}
,
properties 8(b)-8(e) of Proposition
5.13

produce corresponding properties (I1)’-(I4)’ for
{\lambda}_{2,n}F_{n}
and

n\in\mathbb{N}
large, in particular, we find
e_{2}
multi-graphical annuli

\widetilde{G}_{2,n}(1),\ldots,\widetilde{G}_{2,n}(e_{2})
in

({\lambda}_{2,n}F_{n})(\Delta_{n}(4R)\setminus\Delta_{n}(R/2))
(this
R>0

is now introduced in item 8 of Proposition
5.13
).

Definition 5.15
.

Define
r_{2,n}
as the supremum of the extrinsic radii
r\geq 4R/{\lambda}_{2,n}
such that annular enlargements
\widehat{G}_{2,n}(j)
of the

\widetilde{G}_{2,n}(j)
satisfying conditions (B1),(B2),(B3)
of Lemma
4.1
for the choices
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
,
inner extrinsic radius
R_{1}=\frac{R}{2{\lambda}_{2,n}}
,
outer extrinsic radius
R_{2}=r_{2,n}
and angle
{\alpha}={\alpha}_{1}
.

As we did in Remark
5.9
, we next discuss whether or not

r_{2,n}
tends to zero as
n\to\infty
. If
\{r_{2,n}\}_{n}
is bounded
away from zero with this bound independent of the sequence

\{F_{n}\}_{n}\subset\Lambda
, then Proposition
5.16

below holds with
s_{0}=2
. Otherwise, we repeat the process in steps (M1)
and (M2) above for the sequence
\frac{1}{r_{2,n}}F_{n}
and find a
complete, finitely branched minimal immersion
f_{3}\colon\Sigma_{3}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

with finite total curvature which is a limit of (a subsequence of) the

{\lambda}_{3,n}F_{n}
, where
{\lambda}_{3,n}=\frac{1}{r_{2,n}}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This process of finding scales
\{{\lambda}_{s,n}\}_{n}
and limits
f_{s}

(
s=1,2,\ldots
) must stop after a finite
number
s_{0}
of times (
s_{0}\leq I_{0}+1
), because each time we apply the
process we find
\Delta
-type components
in (a subsequence of)
\{F_{n}\}_{n}
with strictly larger index
by item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13
, but the index
of each
F_{n}
is at most
I_{0}+1
. This implies that
r_{s_{0},n}
is
bounded away from zero, with the lower bound being independent of the
sequence
\{F_{n}\}_{n}\subset\Lambda
. In this setting, the discussion in
item 2(A) of Remark
5.9
implies that Proposition
5.3

holds for the scale of
f_{s_{0}}\colon\Sigma_{s_{0}}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
More precisely:

Proposition 5.16
.

There exists
{\delta}_{4}\in(0,{\delta}_{3}]
(this
{\delta}_{3}\in(0,{\delta}_{2}]
was given in
Definition
4.5
for the choices
m=3(I_{0}+1)+3

and
L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
) such that the hypotheses of
Lemma
4.1
hold for annular
enlargements
\widehat{G}_{s_{0},n}(j)
of the multi-graphs
\widetilde{G}_{s_{0},n}(j)

(here
j=1,\ldots,e_{s_{0}}
with
e_{s_{0}}
being the number of ends of

f_{s_{0}}
) between the geodesic spheres in
X

centered at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
of extrinsic inner radius

\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2{\lambda}_{s_{0}}(n)}

and extrinsic outer radius
{\delta}_{4}
, and with the choice
{\alpha}=\tau_{1}

for hypotheses (B1), (B2)
(this
\tau_{1}\in(0,{\alpha}_{1}]
was also introduced in
Definition
4.5
).

With Proposition
5.16
at hand, we define

{\delta}:={\delta}_{4}/2,\quad{\delta}_{1}={\delta}/2,

(5.25)

(here
{\delta}_{4}\in(0,{\delta}_{3}]
is given by Proposition
5.16
).
We are now ready to achieve the main goal of Section
5.5
.

Proposition 5.17
.

Items 1, 2, 3 of Theorem
1.2
hold in the case
I=I_{0}+1

for immersions in
\Lambda_{t}
, for some

t>\widehat{C}_{s}({\delta}_{1}/2)
sufficiently large.

Proof.

The idea is to adapt appropriately the arguments at the end of
Section
5.4.2
(after Definition
5.4
).
Pick a smallest
R_{s_{0}}>0
so that items (H0)’-…-(H4)’ hold
with

replaced by

and with the same value

(also see items 8(b)-…-8(e) of Proposition

for the particular case

).
In particular, item (H5)’ can be also adapted to
f_{s_{0}}
after applying
the estimate (

) in Proposition

with

and

(this is the total branching order of

by (

));
equivalently, we can adapt item 4(c) of Lemma

to

and conclude the following estimate:

(H5)”

Given

, the intrinsic distance in the pullback
metric by
f_{s_{0}}
from
\vec{0}\in\Sigma_{s_{0}}
to any point in the

is at most

,
where

can be bounded from above depending only on

;
in fact,

where

is defined in item 2 of Proposition

.

Define
{\Delta}_{s_{0},n}(R_{s_{0}})\subset M_{n}
as the component of

({\lambda}_{s_{0},n}F_{n})^{-1}\left({\lambda}_{s_{0},n}\overline{B}_{X}(F_{n}(p_{1}(n)),{\textstyle\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{{\lambda}_{s_{0},n}}})\right)

that contains
p_{1}(n)
. Reasoning as when we deduced (I5)’
from (H5)’ and (J5)’ from (H5)’, we have the
following adaptation of (J5)’ to this setting:

(J5)”

The intrinsic distance in the pullback metric by

on
M_{n}
,
from

to the boundary of

(here,

is introduced in (H5)”
above).

Take
t
large enough such that

(K1)’

\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{t}[a(I_{0})+1]\leq\frac{{\delta}_{1}}{10}
.

(K2)’

The description in items (J1)’-…-(J5)’ holds for
F_{n}
with

e=e_{s_{0}}
being the number of ends of
f_{s_{0}}
and

L_{0}=3\pi(I_{0}+2)+1
.

Define
A_{1}:=t
and
r_{F}(1):={\delta}_{1}
.

Given

,

where the maximum of

in

, see

.

Figure 4:
Schematic (non-proportional) representation of the extrinsic

Next we prove item

for

.
Let

using
S_{F}(\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})
to denote the extrinsic geodesic

with radius
\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t}
, that

\begin{array}[]{rclr}d_{M}(p_{1},q)&\leq&{\displaystyle\max_{x\in\partial\Delta_{1}\cap F^{-1}(S_{F}(\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t}))}}d_{M}(p_{1},x)+d_{M}(\Delta_{1}\cap F^{-1}(S_{F}(\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})),q)&\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&\leq&\frac{1}{t}[a(I_{0})+1]R_{s_{0}}+d_{M}(\Delta_{1}\cap F^{-1}(S_{F}(\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})),q)&\mbox{(by (J5)'')}\\
&\leq&\frac{1}{t}[a(I_{0})+1]R_{s_{0}}+\sqrt{1+\frac{\tau^{2}}{3}}(r_{F}(1)-\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})&\mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{lemma5.3})}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&\leq&\frac{{\delta}_{1}}{10}+\sqrt{1+\frac{\tau^{2}}{3}}r_{F}(1)&\mbox{(by (K1)')}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&=&\left(\frac{1}{10}+\sqrt{1+\frac{\tau^{2}}{3}}\right)r_{F}(1)&\\
&<&\frac{5}{4}r_{F}(1).&\mbox{(because $\tau\leq\pi/10$)}\end{array}

This proves that item 1(a) of Theorem
1.2
holds in the
case
I=I_{0}+1
for
\Delta_{1}
.
To find the remaining
\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{k}

,
we will apply the induction hypothesis
to the restriction of

to

, as an element in a

specified as in Definition

, for some choices of

First observe that the restriction
of

to

is an

-immersion with

that is, condition (A2) in Definition

for

holds for the upper index bound

.

Next we will
explain how to choose the remaining

that determine

in order to apply the induction hypothesis to

as an element in
\Lambda^{\prime}
.

By Proposition
5.16
, we have:

(P1)

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}
be the component of

F^{-1}(\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4}))
that contains

. Then,
the intersection of
F(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1})
with the

between the extrinsic spheres

and
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4})
, consists
of

multi-graphical annuli
\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1),\ldots,\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(e_{s_{0}})
.

In particular, the intrinsic distance between the two boundary curves of
each
\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(h)
,
h\in\{1,\ldots,e_{s_{0}}\}
,
is greater than or equal to
the following positive number independent of

:

{\varepsilon}_{1}:={\delta}_{4}-\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t}.

(5.27)

Observe that taking
{\delta}_{4}
smaller if necessary (this does not
affect the validity of Proposition
5.16
), we can
assume
{\delta}_{4}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}]
. Now, define
{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}={\varepsilon}_{1}
.

Property (P1) implies that the following property hold:

(P2)

The second fundamental form of
F
is uniformly bounded
(independently of
(F\colon M\looparrowright X)\in\Lambda_{t}
)
in

\Delta_{1}\cap F^{-1}\left(\overline{B_{X}}(F(p_{1}),{\delta})\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),\textstyle{\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{t}})\right)

by a constant
A_{1}>0
independent of

. Define

A^{\prime}_{0}=\max\{A_{0},A_{1}\}
.

With the above choices, it follows that the restriction of

to

lies in the collection
\Lambda^{\prime}
introduced
in (
5.26
). By induction hypothesis (with the same choice
of
\tau
, recall that we are proving items 1,2,3 of
Theorem
1.2
by induction on

), we can find

A_{1}^{\prime}\in[A^{\prime}_{0},\infty)
,

{\delta}^{\prime}_{1},{\delta}^{\prime}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}]
(independent of

) with
{\delta}^{\prime}_{1}\leq{\delta}^{\prime}/2
,
and a possibly empty finite collection of points

\mathcal{P}_{F|_{M\setminus\Delta_{1}}}=\{p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{k}\}\subset U(\partial(M\setminus\Delta_{1}),{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{0},\infty)

(5.28)

k\leq I_{0}
, and related numbers

r^{\prime}_{F}(1)>4r^{\prime}_{F}(2)>\ldots>4^{k-1}r^{\prime}_{F}(k),

(5.29)

with
\{r^{\prime}_{F}(1),\ldots,r^{\prime}_{F}(k)\}\subset[{\delta}^{\prime}_{1},\frac{{\delta}^{\prime}}{2}]
and satisfying

1.2
.

Finally, define

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\{t,A^{\prime}_{1}\},\qquad{\delta}=\min\{{\delta}_{4}/2,{\delta}^{\prime}\},\qquad{\delta}_{1}=\min\{{\delta}_{4}/4,{\delta}^{\prime}_{1}\}

(5.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{p_{1},p_{2}=p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p_{k+1}=p^{\prime}_{k}\}\subset U(\partial M,{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{0},\infty),

(5.31)

\displaystyle r_{F}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta}_{4}/4,\quad r_{F}(2)=r^{\prime}_{F}(1),\ldots,\ r_{F}(k+1)=r^{\prime}_{F}(k).

(5.32)

(here
{\delta}_{4}
is the number defined in Proposition
5.16
,
and
t
was defined just after item (J5)”;
observe that we do not lose generality by assuming that

r_{F}(1)>4r^{\prime}_{F}(1)
)).
Also notice that the points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k+1}
belong to

U(\partial M,{\varepsilon}_{0},\infty)
(compare to (

) and to the
statement of Theorem
1.2
): the reason for this is that

|A_{F}|(p_{j})|>A_{0}^{\prime}\geq A_{1}
for each
j=1,\ldots,k+1
.

Now, its clear that
items 1, 2, 3 of Theorem
1.2
hold for
I=I_{0}+1
with the
exception of the first statement of item
1
(c) for
i=1

and
j\in\{2,\ldots,k+1\}
, that we prove next.
To conclude that
B_{M}(p_{1},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(1))\cap B_{M}(p_{j},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j))=\varnothing
,
first note that
\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(1)=\frac{7}{20}{\delta}_{4}<\frac{1}{2}{\delta}_{4}
,
hence it suffices to show that
B_{M}(p_{j},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j))

does not intersect
F^{-1}\left[\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4})\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4}/2)\right]
. Arguing by contradiction,
suppose that there exists a point
q\in B_{M}(p_{j},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j))

such that
F(q)\in\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4})\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4}/2)
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{rclr}{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{0}&\leq&d_{M}(p_{j},\partial\Delta_{1})&\mbox{(by
(\ref{eq:PFrest}) and (\ref{eq:PF}))}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&\leq&d_{M}(p_{j},q)+d_{M}(q,\partial\Delta_{1})&\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&<&\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j)+d_{M}(q,\partial\Delta_{1})&\mbox{(because $q\in B_{M}(p_{j},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j))$)}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&\leq&\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j)+\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}/3}\,{\delta}_{4}/4&\mbox{(by item (C2) of Lemma~\ref{lemma5.3}, see $(\star)$ below)}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&<&\frac{7}{20}r_{F}(1)+\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}/3}\,{\delta}_{4}/4&\mbox{(by (\ref{eq:r_Forder}))}\\
\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}&=&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{7}{20}+\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}/3}\right){\delta}_{4},&\mbox{(by (\ref{eq:defrF}))}\end{array}

where in
(\star)
we have used that
F(\partial\Delta_{1})\subset\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4}/4)
and
F(q)\notin B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4}/2)
. Hence it suffices to show that
the inequality

{\delta}_{4}-\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{ve1})}}{{=}}{\varepsilon}_{1}={\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{0}<\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{7}{20}+\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}/3}\right){\delta}_{4}

leads to contradiction. Manipulating the last inequality, it is clearly
equivalent to

\left[1-\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{7}{20}+\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}/3}\right)\right]{\delta}_{4}<\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\footnotesize(K1)'}}}{{\leq}}\frac{{\delta}_{1}}{10}\frac{5}{a(I_{0})+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{de,de1})}}{{=}}\frac{{\delta}_{4}}{8}\frac{1}{a(I_{0})+1},

Therefore,

B_{M}(p_{1},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(1))\cap B_{M}(p_{j},\frac{7}{5}r_{F}(j))=\varnothing

for
i=1
and
j\in\{2,\ldots,k+1\}
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
5.17
.
∎

Recall that the domains
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1}(n)\subset M_{n}

are defined in the proof of Proposition
5.17
and
each such domain is geometrically the component of
p_{1}=p_{1}(n)
in the
preimage by
F=F_{n}
of an extrinsic ball in
X=X_{n}

centered at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
of a small radius
r_{F}(1)={\delta}_{1}
independent of
n
.
For future referencing in the definition of “the hierarchy structure of
\Delta_{1}
”
appearing in the next section, we make the following definition.

Definition 5.18
.

Suppose that the number of
ascending levels
s_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
in the construction of
\Delta_{1}(n)

satisfies
s_{0}>1
. In this case, for each
i\in\{2,\ldots,s_{0}\}
,
we define the related sets:

1.

Q_{2}(n)\subset M_{n}
(defined in Proposition
5.13
), which satisfy:

(a)

Q_{2}(n)
contains
p_{1}(n)
and its finite cardinality is
independent of
n

and at most
I
.

(b)

The norms of the second fundamental
forms of the immersions
\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright\frac{1}{r_{n}}X_{n}

have local maxima at points in
Q_{2}(n)
that are blowing-up
as
n\to\infty
.

(c)

The points in
Q_{2}(n)
stay at a uniform distance at most
R_{0,2}

(this is the constant
R_{0}
appearing in the main statement of Proposition
5.13
)
from the points
p_{1}(n)
in the metric of
M_{n}
induced by

\frac{1}{r_{n}}F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright\frac{1}{r_{n}}X_{n}
, and these points stay at
a uniform distance greater than some
{\varepsilon}_{2,2}>0

(called
{\varepsilon}_{2}>0
in Proposition
5.13
)
from each other.

For
i\in\{3,\ldots,s_{0}\}
,

Q_{i}(n)\subset M_{n}
are the similarly defined finite
sets in
M_{n}
with related positive numbers
R_{0,i}
,

{\varepsilon}_{2,i}
, with respect to rescalings of the immersions
F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright X_{n}
.
Furthermore, for
i\neq i^{\prime}\in\{2,\ldots,s_{0}\}
,
Q_{i}(n)\cap Q_{i^{\prime}}(n)=\{p_{1}(n)\}
,
and so, each of the sets
Q_{i}(n)
contains the point
p_{1}(n)
.

2.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{2}\subset\Sigma_{2}
is defined in item 6(a) of Proposition
5.13

(it was called
\mathcal{S}(f_{2})
there).
For
i\in\{3,\ldots,s_{0}\}
, the sets
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
are defined in the similar manner.

5.6
Counting index, genus and total spinning for local
hierarchies

In Section
5.5
we have explained a process of going “up” in
finding scales and limits with center
p_{1}(n)
, so that after

s_{0}\leq I_{0}+1
steps, we finish the “ascending” process and define the final

\Delta
-piece containing
p_{1}(n)

(called
\Delta_{1}
in the proof of Proposition
5.17
).
Throughout this ascending process, we have found other points
occurring inside
\Delta_{1}
where the
second fundamental form can blow up; we will refer to
these blow-up points as
q
-points in
\Delta_{1}
(these
q
-points that lie in
the sets
Q_{i}(n)\subset M_{n}
described in item 1 of Definition
5.18

and produce corresponding sets
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
,
i=2\ldots,s_{0}
, described in
item 2 of Definition
5.18
). It is crucial to remark that
the compact piece
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1}(n)
occurs in a sequence of
immersions
F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright X_{n}
, while its topological and geometric
structure also depends on the complete,
possibly branched minimal surfaces
which are limits obtained after blowing up
\Delta_{1}(n)

around its
q
-points.

In order to understand the structure of the piece
\Delta_{1}
(i.e.,
to prove the estimates in items B, C, D of Theorem
1.2
),
we must analyze how the related
\Delta
-pieces around these
q
-points
affect the geometry of
\Delta_{1}
. This analysis will be done
by going “down levels” in
\Delta_{1}
: we will first analyze
the
q
-points in
Q_{s_{0}}(n)
, i.e., those
q
-points
occurring at the level of the limit

f_{s_{0}}
(this is the
top level
of the piece
\Delta_{1}

in the language introduced in Section
5.6.1
below),
and subsequently go to lower levels which occur at every

q
-point not being a minimal element in the sense of
Definition
5.21
below.
The notion of
hierarchy
of
\Delta_{1}
(Definition
5.23
)
will encompass all
q
-points at different levels and the related

\Delta
-type pieces around them.
The way that this hierarchy affects some quantities
appearing in items B, C, D of Theorem
1.2
(like index,
genus, number of boundary components, total spinning along the
boundary, etc) is encoded in Theorem
5.27
below,
which is an inequality that generalizes the Chodosh-Maximo
estimate (
3.5
) to the new framework of hierarchies.
Although it is premature at this point for the reader to fully understand
what is meant by a hierarchy, we suggest that the reader frequently
check his/her developing understanding of this concept
by referring to the schematic Figure
5
below,
which represents a particular example
of a hierarchy; also see Example
5.19
and item 3 in
Example
5.24
for further explanations of this example.

In the remainder of this section,
|X|
will denote the number of elements of a
finite set
X
, and if
X
is a topological space with finitely many
connected components,
\#_{c}(X)
will denote the number of these
components.

5.6.1
The hierarchy associated to a
\Delta
-type
piece

Let
\{F_{n}\}_{n}
be a sequence in the space

\Lambda=\Lambda(I_{0},H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})

with second fundamental form not uniformly bounded. Let

\Delta=\Delta(n)
be the
connected, compact surface that arises around an initial blow-up point

p(n)\in M_{n}
for
n
large (this is a
\Delta
-piece, in the language
of the first two paragraphs of Section
5.6
).
Recall that the construction given
in Section
5.5
performs finitely many blow-ups centered at the

p(n)
, giving rise to
s_{0}

stages

(f_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i},\{{\lambda}_{i,n}\}_{n})
,
i=1,\ldots,s_{0}

described in items (S1)-(S2) below.

(S1)

f_{i}\colon\Sigma_{i}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a (possibly finitely connected)
complete minimal surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with finite total curvature
that passes through the origin, possibly with a finite number of
branch points and possibly with non-orientable components.

\Sigma_{1}
is connected and
f_{1}\colon\Sigma_{1}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
unbranched and non-flat, but for
i=2,\ldots,s_{0}
,

f_{i}
could have flat components with or without
branch points, in the sense that the image set of the related branched
immersion lies in a flat plane
(that could fail to pass through the origin).

(S2)

{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
is a finite subset
(
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\varnothing
) and
\{{\lambda}_{i,n}\}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
is
a sequence diverging to
\infty
such that (see Section
5.5.3
)

(a)

\{{\lambda}_{i,n}F_{n}\}_{n}
converges to
f_{i}
in
\Sigma_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
as
n\to\infty
.

(b)

\{{\lambda}_{i,n}F_{n}\}_{n}
fails to have bounded
second fundamental form around each point of
Q_{i}(n)
(this is the set
introduced in Definition
5.18
, which gives rise to
\mathcal{S}_{i}
).

(c)

{\lambda}_{i,n}/{\lambda}_{i+1,n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
for each
i=1,\ldots,{s_{0}}-1
.

Because of properties (S2.a), (S2.b), we will refer to

\mathcal{S}_{i}
as the
singular set of convergence
of
{\lambda}_{i,n}F_{n}

to
f_{i}
.

Example 5.19
.

We will illustrate the above description with an example based on
Figure
5
. The blue circle around
\Delta_{q_{1,1}}
represents a
compact
\Delta
-piece of
{\lambda}_{1,n}F_{n}
based at the blow-up points
q_{1,1}(n)\in M_{n}

which resembles arbitrarily well
(for
n
large) the intersection of the first stage limit
f_{1}\colon\Sigma_{1}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
introduced in item (S1) with a ball of large radius centered at
the origin; the ascending blue straight line segment connecting the
blue circle around
\Delta_{q_{1,1}}
with the red circle around

\Delta_{q_{1}}
represents a component
W_{1}^{\prime}
of the second stage limit

f_{2}\colon\Sigma_{2}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

which contains at least one point in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
obtained as a blow-down
limit (by scale
\frac{{\lambda}_{2,n}}{{\lambda}_{1,n}}\to 0
) of the
\Delta
-piece

\Delta_{q_{1,1}}
in
{\lambda}_{2,n}F_{n}
. In fact, each end of
f_{1}
is a
multi-graph outside of a ball of some finite multiplicity
m_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
,
such an end produces a branch point for
f_{2}
of multiplicity
m_{1}-1
, and
the number of leaves of
f_{2}
passing through the image of such a branch point is
at least equal to the number of ends of
f_{1}
.
The red circle around
\Delta_{q_{1}}
represents a compact
\Delta
-piece
of
{\lambda}_{2,n}F_{n}
which resembles arbitrarily well (for
n
large) the
intersection of
f_{2}(\Sigma_{2})
with a ball of large radius centered at the
origin; the ascending red straight line segment connecting the red circle
around
\Delta_{q_{1}}
to the black circle around
\Delta
represents a
component
W_{1}
of the third stage limit
f_{3}\colon\Sigma_{3}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

which contains a point in
\mathcal{S}_{3}
obtained as a blow-down limit
(by scale
\frac{{\lambda}_{3,n}}{{\lambda}_{2,n}}\to 0
) of the
\Delta
-piece

\Delta_{q_{1}}
inside
{\lambda}_{3,n}F_{n}
. Similarly as before,
each end of
f_{2}
is a multi-graph outside of a ball
of some finite multiplicity
m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, this end produces a branch point
for
f_{3}
of multiplicity
m_{2}-1
, and the number of leaves of
f_{3}

passing through such a branch point is at least equal to the number
of ends of
f_{2}
. The black circle around
\Delta

represents the final compact
\Delta
-piece of
{\lambda}_{3,n}F_{n}
, i.e.,
Proposition
5.16
holds with
s_{0}=3
for this ”ascending”
linear subgraph starting at
\Delta_{q_{1,1}}
and finishing at
\Delta
.
If we start ascending from
\Delta_{q_{1,2}}
instead of from

\Delta_{q_{1,1}}
, we will find again
s_{0}=3
(although the stage
limits are different than before, since the rescaling
is centered at a different blow-up sequence in
M_{n}
)
but if we start ascending from
\Delta_{q_{2}}

(resp. from
\Delta_{q_{3,1,1}}
) we will find
s_{0}=2
(resp.
s_{0}=4
).
Both
W_{1}^{\prime}
and the T-shaped polygon
W_{2}^{\prime}
connecting the blue circles around

\Delta_{q_{1,1}},\Delta_{q_{1,2}}
with the red circle around

\Delta_{q_{1}}
represent that
\Sigma_{2}
has two components,
each one with its own number of ends, and that each of these
ends possibly produce branch points in
\mathcal{S}_{3}
as explained above.
We will continue with explaining aspects of this Figure
5

in Example
5.20
.

We now come back to the general description with the notation in (S1)-(S2)
and in Definition
5.18
.
The
hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
of
\Delta
decomposes
into finitely many
levels,
which are defined
recursively as follows, starting from what we will call the

top level
of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
. There exists a possibly disconnected
complete, branched minimal
immersion
f_{\rm T}\colon\Sigma_{\rm T}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
(the subindex
{\rm T}

stands for top; in the notation in (S1)-(S2),
f_{\rm T}=f_{s_{0}}
), such
that the convergence of portions of suitable expansions

{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}={\lambda}_{s_{0},n}F_{n}
of
F_{n}
to
f_{\rm T}

is smooth away from a finite singular set of convergence

\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}\subset\Sigma_{\rm T}

(
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}
could be empty), and the second
fundamental forms of
{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}
fail to be bounded around (extrinsically) each point
q\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}
;
suppose that such a point
q
corresponds to
a sequence
\{q(n)\}_{n}
with
q(n)\in Q_{\rm T}(n)\subset M_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large.
This means that
\{{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}(q(n))\}_{n}

converges to
f_{\rm T}(q)
(in harmonic coordinates
of radius
R_{0,{\rm T}}
centered at
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
, where
R_{0,{\rm T}}
is defined in item 1(c)
of Definition
5.18
) and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup\left\{|A_{{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}}|(x)\ :\ x\in B_{{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)X_{n}}(F_{n}(q(n)),1/m)\right\}=\lim_{n\to\infty}|A_{{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}}|(q(n))=\infty,

for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large.
Moreover:

(T1)

f_{\rm T}
is unbranched away from
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}
.

(T2)

The number of ends
e(\Sigma_{\rm T})
of
\Sigma_{\rm T}

(resp. the total spinning at infinity
S(f_{\rm T})
of

f_{\rm T}
) equals the number of boundary components of
\Delta

(resp. total spinning
S(\Delta)
of
\Delta
along
\partial\Delta
):

e(\Sigma_{\rm T})=\#_{c}(\partial\Delta):=e(\Delta),\qquad S(f_{\rm T})=S(\Delta).

(5.33)

Let
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}
be the set of components of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
.

We next make a similar quotient space of the abstract surface
\Sigma_{\rm T}

of this branched immersion
f_{\rm T}
as the one in
Lemma
5.14
,
thereby defining a quotient space
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
,
a related quotient map
\pi\colon\Sigma_{\rm T}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
, and
a singular set

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\pi(\mathcal{S}_{\rm T})

defined as in Lemma
5.14
. Observe that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|=|Q_{\rm T}(n)|

(which is independent of
n
). Given
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, let

\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}(q)=\pi^{-1}(q)\subset\mathcal{S}_{\rm T})
. Thus, every point in

\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}(q)
identifies to the point
q
in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
,
and every other point of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
only identifies with itself.
After endowing
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
with the quotient topology,

\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
becomes a path-connected metric space,
and
f_{\rm T}\colon\Sigma_{\rm T}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
induces a well-defined
continuous map, denoted also by
f_{\rm T}\colon\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

with a slight abuse of notation.
Observe that
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
has the induced
structure of a (smooth) Riemannian surface, and that
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
is a topological
surface in a small neighborhood
of a given point
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
if and only if

\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}(q)
consists of a single point. Also, the restriction of

f_{\rm T}
to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}

is a minimal immersion with finite total curvature in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is
complete away from its limit point set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{T}
.

Example 5.20
.

As announced in Example
5.19
, we continue to explain some aspects
in Figure
5
. The red component
W_{1}
of
\Sigma_{3}
connects to the
red circles around
\Delta_{q_{1}},\Delta_{q_{2}}
,
meaning that
W_{1}
contains at least two
distinct points in
\mathcal{S}_{3}
which lead to two distinct points
q_{1},q_{2}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{3}
. The blue component
W_{2}^{\prime}
of
\Sigma_{2}
connects to the red
circle around
\Delta_{q_{1}}
and to the blue circles around

\Delta_{q_{1,1}},\Delta_{q_{1,2}}
, meaning that
W_{2}^{\prime}
contains at least
two distinct points in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
which produce distinct points
q_{1,1},q_{1,2}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, in contrast to the blue component
W_{1}^{\prime}

of
\Sigma_{2}
, whose points in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
only give rise to one point in

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, namely
q_{1,1}
.

We now return to the general situation. Given
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
,
for all
n
sufficiently large we can find a related compact,
connected piece
\Delta_{q}=\Delta_{q}(n)\subset M_{n}

satisfying item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13

for
\widetilde{F}_{n}={\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)F_{n}
.

The index of
\Delta_{q}
is
strictly less than the index of
\Delta
(this is clear in the case that

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus\{q\}\neq\varnothing
; in the case that

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\{q\}
we have that

f_{\rm T}
cannot be flat, since this corresponds to the case
J=1

in item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13
), and so,
we can apply Lemma
3.4
to conclude that
f_{\rm T}
is
not stable, which gives
Index
(\Delta)\geq\mbox{ Index}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\mbox{ Index}(\Delta_{q})>\mbox{ Index}(\Delta_{q})
.
For different points
q,q^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
,
the corresponding compact domains
\Delta_{q(n)},\Delta_{q^{\prime}(n)}\subset M_{n}

are disjoint.

Let

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}=\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}(n)=\{\Delta_{q}=\Delta_{q(n)}\subset M_{n}\ |\ q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\},

Given
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, let
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(q)
be the (finite) set of
components of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
such that each
