W\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(q)
contains
at least one point of
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}(q)=\pi^{-1}(q)
.
We can choose a finite collection

\mathcal{D}_{q}
of sufficiently small (possibly branched)
stable
minimal
disks in
\Sigma_{\rm T}
centered at the points in
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}(q)
,
such that

(U4)

For each component
W
of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
, it holds

I(W)=I(W\setminus\cup_{q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{T}}\mathcal{D}_{q})
.

(U5)

The set

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{c}:=\bigcup_{q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}}\mathcal{D}_{q}\subset\Sigma_{\rm T}

(5.34)

is contained in the limit as
n\to\infty
of

{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}(n)
.

Let

\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}=\Sigma_{\rm T}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{c}.

(5.35)

Property (U4) implies that the index
I(\Sigma_{\rm T})=I(\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c})
.
Note that the number of components
\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})=\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c})
,
since removing an interior disk from a connected surface does not
disconnect it.

Definition 5.21
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}=\varnothing
in the situation above, then

\Sigma_{\rm T}
consists of a single non-flat, connected, unbranched minimal
surface with finite total curvature. In this case, we say that the
hierarchy
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
of
\Delta
is
trivial

(with no levels) and that
\Delta
is a
minimal element
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}\neq\varnothing
, then we
define the
top level
of
\Delta=\Delta(n)
(for
n
large)
as the triple
(\bf\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T},\mathcal{V}_{\rm T},\mathcal{W}_{\rm T})
.
In this case, we can apply for each
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}

the above description to the corresponding compact domain
\Delta_{q}

(exchange
\Delta
by
\Delta_{q}
), which produces the
triple
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{V}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{W}_{{\rm T}(q)})

associated to
\Delta_{q}
with top level
{\rm T}(q)
.
As before, we have two cases:

•

If
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)}=\varnothing
for a point

q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, then the hierarchy of
\Delta_{q}
is trivial and

\Delta_{q}
is called a
minimal element

(for instance, in Figure
5
, the minimal elements are

\Delta_{q_{2}},\Delta_{q_{1,1}},\Delta_{q_{1,2}},\Delta_{q_{3,1,1}}
, which
have associated number of stages
s_{0}(q_{2})=2
,

s_{0}(q_{1,1})=s_{0}(q_{1,2})=3
,
s_{0}(q_{3,1,1})=4
;
observe that the number of stages is not defined for the
\Delta_{q}
-pieces
which are not minimal elements).

•

If
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)}\neq\varnothing
,
we say that the corresponding top level
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{V}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{W}_{{\rm T}(q)})
of
\Delta_{q}
is a

level of the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)

different from its top level, and proceed
recursively. Let us denote by
L\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
the number of these
levels of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
;
see Figure
5
for the schematic representation of a hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
with four levels.

Remark 5.22
.

1.

Observe that the notion of level only makes sense provided that

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\neq\varnothing
.

2.

This recursive process of assigning levels to
\Delta

(not being a minimal element) is finite, since each
\Delta_{q}
has
non-zero index, which can be realized by a compact unstable domain
in
M_{n}
for
n
large, and the related compact unstable
domains for different
q
-points in the same level of
\Delta

can be taken pairwise disjoint (recall that the index of
F_{n}
was
assumed to be less than or equal to some bound
I_{0}
independent of
n
).

3.

This recursive process of assigning levels to
\Delta

(not being a minimal element) is finite. In fact, it follows
from the arguments used to prove item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13
that the index increases
each time we add a level, and so
L+1\leq I(\Delta)
.

Definition 5.23
.

We define the
singular set

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
as the union
of all the singular sets
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)}
for singular points of
previously defined levels (including
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
).
Similarly, we let
\mathcal{S}
be the union of all
\mathcal{S}_{{\rm T}(q)}

for singular points of previously defined levels. Let

\mathcal{V}\subset M_{n}
be the union of

\{\Delta\}
together with all the
compact pieces
\Delta_{q}
for singular points of levels of

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be the union of all the
components of related limit surfaces
\Sigma_{{\rm T}(q)}

for singular points of previously defined levels
(including
\Sigma_{\rm T}
). We define the
hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
of
\Delta=\Delta(n)
(for
n
large)
as the triple
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
; and the number

L\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
associated to
\Delta
(see
Definition
5.21
) is called the
number of levels of

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
.

If
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is non-trivial, a compact domain

\Delta_{q}\in\mathcal{V}

(here
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
) is called a
minimal element
of

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
if the hierarchy associated to

\Delta_{q}
is trivial (recall that if
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is trivial,
we called
\Delta
itself a minimal element).

Example 5.24
.

1.

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}=\varnothing
if and only if

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\varnothing
if and
only if the hierarchy of
\Delta
is trivial. In this case,

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}=\{\Sigma_{\rm T}\}
,

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}=\varnothing
,
\mathcal{V}=\{\Delta\}
,
L=0
,
and
\Delta
is a minimal element.

2.

The simplest case of a non-trivial hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is that
of having just one single singular point in its top level (i.e.,

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\{q\}
) and where
\Delta_{q}

has one boundary curve. In this example,

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}=\{\Delta_{q}\}
,
\mathcal{V}=\{\Delta,\Delta_{q}\}
,

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}
consists of a
single, non-flat (non-flatness of this single component of
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}

follows from the proof of item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13
),
connected, complete minimal surface
\Sigma_{\rm T}
with finite total
curvature and a unique branch point at
q
with branching order at least
two,
\mathcal{W}=\{\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{\rm T}\}
where

\Sigma_{1}
is a non-flat, connected, complete minimal immersion
(no branch points) with
finite total curvature, the number of levels is
L=1
,
and
\Delta_{q}
is a minimal element.

3.

See Figure
5
for an example of a hierarchy with four
levels. In this example,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q_{1})}=\{q_{1,1},q_{1,2}\}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q_{3})}=\{q_{3,1}\}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q_{3,1})}=\{q_{3,1,1}\}
.
The minimal elements of this hierarchy are
\Delta_{q_{2}},\Delta_{q_{1,1}},\Delta_{q_{1,2}},\Delta_{q_{3,1,1}}
. The surface
\Sigma_{\rm T}

has two (possibly) branched components
W_{1},W_{2}
, and the

\pi
-projection of the set of branch points of
W_{1}
is contained in
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
, while

\pi
-projection of the set of branch points of
W_{2}

is contained in
\{q_{2},q_{3}\}
.
Observe that in this example
\Delta_{q_{2}}
has at least two boundary components
(for
n
large), one component which corresponds to the boundary
of a possibly branched minimal disk
in the limit branched minimal surface
W_{1}
and another component which
corresponds to the boundary of a possibly branched minimal disk in the limit
W_{2}
.

Figure 5:
Schematic representation of a hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
with four levels (top level in red, other levels in
blue, green and purple.

\mathcal{V}
can be equipped with the following partial order:
given
\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
, we denote

\Delta^{\prime}\preceq\Delta^{\prime\prime}
if
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus,
\Delta_{q}\preceq\Delta
for every
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, and

\Delta_{q}\in\mathcal{V}
is a minimal element of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)

precisely when
\Delta_{q}
is minimal with respect to the partial order

\preceq
.

The set
\mathcal{V}
decomposes into

\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{m}\cup\mathcal{V}^{nm},

(5.36)

where
\mathcal{V}^{m}=\{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}\ |\ \Delta^{\prime}\mbox{ is a minimal element}\}

and
\mathcal{V}^{nm}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}^{m}
. Note that
each non-minimal element
\Delta_{q}\in\mathcal{V}^{nm}
with

q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
creates a level of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
below it with respect to
\preceq

(namely, its top level
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{V}_{{\rm T}(q)},\mathcal{W}_{{\rm T}(q)})
).
Assuming that
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is non-trivial,
all the levels of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
except for the
top one are created this way; hence

L=|\mathcal{V}^{nm}|\quad\mbox{ if $\mathcal{H}(\Delta)$
is non-trivial.}

Also, observe that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|+1=|\mathcal{V}^{m}|+|\mathcal{V}^{nm}|

regardless of whether or not
\Delta
is a minimal element.
In particular,
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|\geq L
.

Definition 5.25
.

We define the
excess index
associated to the subset of
minimal elements of
\Delta
as

I^{*}(\mathcal{H})=\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{m}}(I(\Delta^{\prime})-1)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}.

(5.37)

This abstract model of the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
produces a
“decomposition” of the compact domain
\Delta=\Delta(n)\subset M_{n}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
large into compact pieces (in the sense that each piece is a
compact surface with boundary inside
\Delta
, the union of the pieces is

\Delta
and the pieces only intersect along their boundaries): these
pieces correspond to the
\Delta_{q}
with
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}

(observe that
\Delta_{q}=\Delta_{q}(n)
is contained in
M_{n}
),
together with a (finitely connected) compact surface

W(n)\subset\Delta(n)

which is the closure of
\Delta\setminus\left(\bigcup_{q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}}\Delta_{q}\right)
.
Observe that after suitable rescaling by some
{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

diverging to
\infty
, the
{\lambda}_{\rm T}(n)W(n)
converge as
n\to\infty

to the components of the surface
\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}
defined in (
5.35
).

Definition 5.26
.

We define

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\pi^{-1}(q)&\\
\mathcal{W}(\partial=1)&\mbox{set of components $W$ in $\mathcal{W}$ such that
$|W\cap\mathcal{S}|=1$,}\\
\mathcal{W}^{f}&\mbox{set of flat components in $\mathcal{W}$,}\\
\mathcal{W}^{t}=\mathcal{W}(\partial=1)\cap\mathcal{W}^{f}&\mbox{set of {\it trivial} components in $\mathcal{W}$,}\\
\mathcal{W}^{nt}=\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}^{t}&\mbox{set of non-trivial components in $\mathcal{W}$,}\\
\mathcal{W}^{nt,f}&\mbox{set of non-trivial flat components in
$\mathcal{W}$,}\\
\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}=\mathcal{W}^{nt}\setminus\mathcal{W}^{nt,f}&\mbox{set of non-trivial, non-flat components in $\mathcal{W}$, and}\\
\mathcal{W}(\partial>1)=\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}(\partial=1)&\mbox{set of components $W$ in $\mathcal{W}$ such that $|W\cap\mathcal{S}|>1$}.\end{array}\right\}

(5.38)

We will also need the following decomposition of
\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}
:

\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}=\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)\cup\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1),

(5.39)

where
\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)=\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}\cap\mathcal{W}(\partial=1)

(resp.
\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1)=\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}\cap\mathcal{W}(\partial>1)
). In turn, the following decomposition
of
\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1)
will be useful:

\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1)=\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)\cup\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,no}(\partial>1),

(5.40)

where the super-index
or
(orientable),
no
(non-orientable)
refers to the orientability character of each component.

In this paragraph we indicate how the notion of hierarchy arises
in the proof of Structure Theorem
1.2
.
Previously we used the notion of “ascension with

s_{0}
stages” associated to a sequence of points
p_{1}(n)\in M_{n}

with sufficiently large norm of its second fundamental form,
which created a compact piece
\Delta=\Delta_{1}
,
defined just after item (K2)’.
This is the first step in constructing the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
), and
in the previous sections we have proven the following partial result
related to Theorem
1.2
:
For any
(F\colon M\looparrowright X)\in\Lambda=\Lambda(I,H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})
,
there exists a (possibly empty) finite collection
\mathcal{P}_{F}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}\subset U(\partial M,{\varepsilon}_{0},\infty)
of points,
k\leq I
,
numbers
r_{F}(1),\ldots,{r_{F}}(k)\in[{\delta}_{1},\frac{{\delta}}{2}]

with
r_{F}(1)>4r_{F}(2)>\ldots>4^{k-1}r_{F}(k)
and
and related domains
\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}\}
satisfying
items 1, 2, 3, (A) and (E) of Theorem
1.2
,
with respect to some
constant
A_{1}=A_{1}(\Lambda)\in[A_{0},\infty)
.
It remains to prove that
A_{1}=A_{1}(\Lambda)\in[A_{0},\infty)
can also be
chosen sufficiently large so that items (B), (C) and (D) of
Theorem
1.2
also hold for each
\Delta=\Delta_{i}
,

i=1,\ldots,k
. Otherwise,
for some
i=1,\ldots,k
, at least one of the items (B), (C) and (D)
of the theorem fails to hold for
\Delta=\Delta_{i}
;
without loss of generality, assume that
\Delta=\Delta_{1}
.
In this case we may consider
F|_{\Delta}\colon\Delta\looparrowright X
to be an
element in
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda(I,H_{0},{\delta}_{1}/3,A_{1},K_{0})
(regarding the bound
A_{1}

of the second fundamental form of
F|_{\Delta}
in the

\frac{{\delta}_{1}}{3}
-neighborhood of its boundary,
see the two paragraphs just
after Definition
5.4
). The failure of the Structure Theorem
to hold for
\Delta
, no matter how large one chooses
A_{1}
,
leads to a sequence

(F_{n}\colon\Delta(p_{1}(n))\looparrowright X_{n})\in\Lambda(I,H_{0},{\delta}_{1}/3,A_{1},K_{0})
,
where the norm of the second fundamental form of
F_{n}

has a maximum value greater than
n

at
p_{1}(n)\in\Delta
. By our previous arguments,
after replacing by a subsequence,

(F_{n}\colon\Delta(p_{1}(n))\looparrowright X_{n})
leads to the creation of a hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
for
\Delta=\Delta(n)
.
It is this hierarchy that we are referring to in the statement of
Theorem
5.27
below.

The notion of hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
has a good behavior with
respect to proving properties by induction on the number
L
of levels,
which will be the method of proof of Theorem
5.27
below.
Observe that the truncation of a hierarchy
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
with

L\geq 1
levels by simply deleting its top level is again a hierarchy,
with the only difference that the role of
\Delta
is played
by the disjoint
union of the compact pieces
\Delta_{q}
with
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
.
To simplify the notation in the next statement, we will denote again by

\Delta
this disjoint union, and so we will no longer assume that

\Delta
is connected; by hierarchy of such a disconnected
\Delta
,
we mean the union of the hierarchies of the components of
\Delta
.

Theorem 5.27
.

Let
\Delta
be as described previously and be finitely connected.
Then, the index
I(\Delta)
of
\Delta
can be estimated from below by

6\,I(\Delta)\geq-\chi(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)+C(\mathcal{H}),

(5.41)

where
\chi(\Delta)
is the Euler characteristic of
\Delta
,

e(\Delta)=\#_{c}(\partial\Delta)
the number of boundary components,

S(\Delta)
is the total spinning of
\Delta
along its boundary, and
the “correction term”
C(\mathcal{H})
is the following non-negative
integer, which depends on the complexity of the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}
of

\Delta
:

C(\mathcal{H})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|-L+|\mathcal{W}^{nt,f}|+2|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+3|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|,

(5.42)

where
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
is the singular set of the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}
and

L\geq 0
is the number of its levels.
Furthermore, if
\Delta
is connected and has a trivial hierarchy,
then
I^{*}(\mathcal{H})=I(\Delta)-1
,
C(\mathcal{H})=3I(\Delta)-3
,
and so, (
5.41
) reduces to the Chodosh-Maximo
estimate (
3.5
).

Remark 5.28
.

If
\Delta
is orientable, the relation

\chi(\Delta)=2\#_{c}(\Delta)-2g(\Delta)-e(\Delta)

allows us to write (
5.41
) as

6\,I(\Delta)\geq 2g(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+2e(\Delta)-2\#_{c}(\Delta)+C(\mathcal{H}).

(5.43)

Proof of Theorem
5.27
.

First observe that the functions

I(\Delta),\chi(\Delta),S(\Delta),e(\Delta)

are additive on components of
\Delta
. The same holds for

C(\mathcal{H})
, with the understanding that adding components
of
\Delta
also adds the number of levels as well as the other terms
appearing in (
5.42
). Therefore,
(
5.41
) holds if it holds for
connected
\Delta
. The proof of (
5.41
) will be carried out by
induction on the number
L\geq 0
of levels of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
.

Suppose first that
\Delta
is connected and its hierarchy
\mathcal{H}

is trivial. In this case,
L=0
, and
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|=|\mathcal{W}^{nt,f}|=|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|=|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|=0
, hence
C(\mathcal{H})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})=3I(\Delta)-3
,
which reduces (
5.41
) to (
3.5
). This argument also
proves the last statement in the theorem holds.

By the principle of mathematical induction, assume that
L>0
is the
number of levels of
\Delta
and that (
5.41
) holds for (possibly
disconnected)
\Delta^{\prime}
if its hierarchy
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
has less than
L
levels.
Without loss of generality, we will
assume that
\Delta

is connected
. Since
L>0
, then
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)

is non-trivial,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}\neq\varnothing
.

By (
5.36
), the set
\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}
can be written as the
disjoint union

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}=\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm},

(5.44)

where
\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m}=\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}\cap\mathcal{V}^{m}
and

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm}=\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}\cap\mathcal{V}^{nm}
.

In the first paragraph after Definition
5.25
we explained that
for
n
large,
\Delta=\Delta(n)
can be decomposed into the compact
pieces
\Delta_{q}

with
q\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}
and finitely many compact connected domains

W(n)
whose indices are independent of
n
and satisfy

I(W(n))=I(W),

for some component
W\in\mathcal{W}\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}
. This equality,
together with (
5.44
), lead us to the inequality

I(\Delta)\geq I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+I(\Sigma_{\rm T}).

(5.45)

To estimate the first term in the RHS of (
5.45
), we will
apply (
3.5
) to each of the components

\Delta_{q}\in\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m}
(observe that
the total branching number
B
in (
3.5
) vanishes
in our setting), so we get

\displaystyle 6I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})

(5.46)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CM1}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+2S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})-3\#_{c}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{I*}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+2S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m}).

Since the number of levels of the hierarchy for each compact piece

\Delta_{q}
with
q\in\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm}
is less than
L
,
we can estimate the second term in the RHS of (
5.45
) by the
induction hypothesis, hence

6I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})\geq-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+2S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm}),

(5.47)

where
C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})
is the sum of the correction terms

C(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
varying in the hierarchies
of all compact pieces
\Delta_{q}
with

q\in\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm}
.

To estimate the third term in the RHS of (
5.45
),
we will apply (
3.5
) to each of the components
of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
, so we get

3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})\geq-\chi(\Sigma_{\rm T})+2S(f_{\rm T})+e(\Sigma_{\rm T})-2B(\Sigma_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T}),

(5.48)

where
\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})
is the number of flat components of

\Sigma_{\rm T}
(see item 1 of Remark
3.3
).

Thus,

\displaystyle 6I(\Delta)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.7b}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle 6I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+6I(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{X1a},\eqref{X2a},\eqref{STa}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+2S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+2S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})

\displaystyle-\chi(\Sigma_{\rm T})+2S(f_{\rm T})+e(\Sigma_{\rm T})-2B(\Sigma_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T}).

Since
B(\Sigma_{\rm T})=S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})=[S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})-e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})]+[S(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})-e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})]
,
the RHS of the last expression can be written as

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+3e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})

\displaystyle-\chi(\Sigma_{\rm T})+2S(f_{\rm T})+e(\Sigma_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T}).

By using
\chi(\Delta)=\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+\chi(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+\chi(\Sigma_{\rm T})-e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})-e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})
,

e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})=e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})
and
(
5.33
), we can rewrite the last displayed expression as

\displaystyle-\chi(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)

(5.49)

\displaystyle+2e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})

(5.50)

\displaystyle+3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm}).

(5.51)

We next analyze the terms in (
5.50
).

First note that
e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})=\#_{c}(\partial\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c})
,
where
\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}
is the surface defined in (
5.35
).
With this in mind, let
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
denote
the set of components of

\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}
and we obtain the equality:

2\#_{c}(\partial\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})=\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c})).

(5.52)

We will analyze the sum in the RHS of (
5.52
) attending to the
following partition of
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
(compare to (
5.38
)
and (
5.39
)):

(Q1)

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}
is the subset of trivial components
in
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
.

(Q2)

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)
is the subset of
components in
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
that have one boundary curve
and are non-trivial. Equivalently, it is the
subset of components in
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
that have one boundary
curve and are not flat.

(Q3)

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}
is the subset of components in

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
that have more than one boundary curve
and are flat.

(Q4)

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,nf}(\partial>1)
is the subset of
components in
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}
having more than one boundary
curve and which are not flat.

For case (Q1) we have the equality

\displaystyle\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}}(2-3+0)+\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}\right|+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|

(5.53)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-34.14322pt-\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}\right|+\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}\right|+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|=|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|.

Regarding case (Q2), for elements
W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)
we will estimate

I(W^{c})\geq 1
(observe that this inequality holds even if
W^{c}
is
non-orientable, by item 2 of Lemma
3.4
). Therefore,

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)}\hskip-11.38092pt(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))=\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)}\hskip-11.38092pt(2-3+3I(W^{c}))\geq 2\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)\right|.

(5.54)

Cases (Q3) and (Q4) deal with the subset
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
of
components in
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}

having more than one boundary curve. For those, we will show the
following estimate:

Lemma 5.29
.

In the situation above,

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3)\geq|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1.

(5.55)

Let
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
denote the set of components

W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
which have boundary curves on at
least two different components of

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{c}
(defined in (
5.34
)). Then:

1.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|=1
and equality in (
5.55
) holds,
then
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)=\varnothing

(equivalently,
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)
).

2.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|>1
and equality occurs in (
5.55
),
then
\mathcal{Y}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
,
W^{c}
has exactly
two boundary components for each
W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}
, and

|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1
(see Figure
6
).

Figure 6
:
Schematic representation of the top level of a hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)

where equality occurs in (
5.55
). Here,

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}=\{q_{i}\ |\ i=1,\ldots,5\}
,

\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}=\{\Delta_{q_{i}}\ |\ i=1,\ldots,5\}
,

\mathcal{Y}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)=\{W_{1}^{c},W_{2}^{c},W_{3}^{c},W_{4}^{c}\}
,

\Delta_{q_{1}},\Delta_{q_{5}}

both have one boundary curve, while
\Delta_{q_{i}}
(
i=2,3,4
)
have two boundary components each one.

Proof.

Observe that the LHS of (
5.55
) is the sum of a possibly empty set
of positive integers, where we declare this sum to be zero if this set of
positive integers is empty (equivalently,
if
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)=\varnothing
).
Recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\neq\varnothing
.
If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|=1
, then the RHS
of (
5.55
) is zero, hence the inequality (
5.55
)
holds in this case.
If moreover equality holds in (
5.55
),
then
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)=\varnothing
and so,
item 1 of the lemma holds. Hence it remains to prove
(
5.55
) and item 2 of the lemma assuming that

|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|>1
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be the set of components
W
of
\Sigma_{\rm T}
such that

\pi(W)
contains at least two points in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
.
Observe that
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{W}^{nt}\cap W(\partial>1)
and that

W\in\mathcal{Y}\Leftrightarrow W\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}.

Therefore,

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3)\geq\sum_{W\in\mathcal{Y}}(2\#_{c}(\partial[W\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3).

(5.56)

Since
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
is path-connected and
we are assuming that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|>1
, then
for every pair of points

q,q^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, there exists an embedded
path
{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
with
{\gamma}(0)=q,{\gamma}=q^{\prime}
.
In particular,
{\gamma}
contains an embedded open subarc with beginning point

q
and ending point
q^{\prime\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus\{q\}
, such
that for some component
W(q)
of
\mathcal{Y}
, we can view this open subarc as
being contained in the interior of
\pi(W(q))\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
.
In particular,
q\in\pi(W(q))
. Since this holds for every

q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, then we deduce that

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\subset\pi\left(\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{Y}}W\right).

Although
W(q)
might be non-unique,
we will use the axiom of choice to assign a map

q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\mapsto W(q)\in\mathcal{Y}\mbox{ such that }q\in\pi(W(q)).

For
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, let

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q))=\pi(W(q))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}.

Thus,
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q))|\geq 2
for each
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
.

Notice that for each
q^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q))
,
W(q)\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}
contains at least one boundary curve in

\partial\mathcal{D}_{q^{\prime}}

(recall that
\mathcal{D}_{q^{\prime}}
was defined right before (
5.34
)). Hence,

\#_{c}(\partial[W(q)\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])\geq|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q))|.

(5.57)

We will construct
l\in\mathbb{N}
points

q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
, such that

q_{i+1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus[\cup_{j=1}^{i}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{j}))]

and
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\ldots\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{l}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
.
Choose an arbitrary
q_{1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
with a related

W(q_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}
. Since
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|\geq 2
,

\displaystyle 2\#_{c}(\partial[W(q_{1})\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.19}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle 2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|-3=(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|-1)+(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|-2)

(5.58)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|-1.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
, then
l=1
in our
construction of points and (
5.55
) follows from (
5.56
)
and (
5.58
).

Suppose
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))|<|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
.
Since
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}

is path-connected, there exists a shortest embedded arc
{\alpha}_{1}
in

\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
from
\pi(W(q_{1}))

to the finite set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))

with one of its end points being some
q_{2}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))
and its other end point in

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))
.
In particular,
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|\geq 1
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}(2\#_{c}(\partial[W(q_{i})\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.19}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}(2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

(5.59)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|-1)

\displaystyle\hskip-119.50148pt+(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|-1)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-2).

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
, then

l=2
in our construction of points and (
5.55
) follows
from (
5.56
) and (
5.59
).

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))|<|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
, then
there exists a shortest embedded arc
{\alpha}_{2}
in
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\rm T}
from

\pi(W(q_{1}))\cup\pi(W(q_{2}))
to the finite set

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus[\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))]

with one of its end points being some
q_{3}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\setminus[\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))]

and its other end point in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))
.
In particular,
|[\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))]\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{3}))|\geq 1
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}(2\#_{c}(\partial[W(q_{i})\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.19}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}(2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

(5.60)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt\sum_{i=1}^{3}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|+\sum_{i=1}^{3}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt(|\cup_{i=1}^{3}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-1)+(|[\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cup\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2}))]\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{3}))|-1)

\displaystyle\hskip-221.93158pt+

\displaystyle\hskip-122.34692pt(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{1}))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{2})|-1))+\sum_{i=1}^{3}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-2).

If
|\cup_{i=1}^{3}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
,
then
l=3
in our construction of points and (
5.55
)
follows from (
5.56
) and (
5.60
).

If
|\cup_{i=1}^{3}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|<|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
,
then we repeat the above process finitely many times
(because
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
is finite),
finding points
q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}
such that

|(\cup_{i=1}^{j-1}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i})))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{j})))|\geq 1
, for each
j=2,\ldots,l
, and

|\cup_{i=1}^{l}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l}(2\#_{c}(\partial[W(q_{i})\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.19}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l}(2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

(5.61)

\displaystyle\hskip-253.22934pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-139.41832pt\sum_{i=1}^{l}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|+\sum_{i=1}^{l}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-3)

\displaystyle\hskip-253.22934pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-139.41832pt(|\cup_{i=1}^{l}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-1)+\sum_{j=2}^{l}(|(\cup_{i=1}^{j-1}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i})))\cap\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{j}))|-1)

\displaystyle\hskip-139.41832pt+\sum_{i=1}^{l}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|-2).

As
|\cup_{i=1}^{l}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|
,
then (
5.55
) follows from (
5.56
) and (
5.61
).

If equality in (
5.55
) occurs, then equality in (
5.56
) implies
that
\mathcal{Y}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
or equivalently,

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(\partial>1)=\mathcal{W}(\partial>1)\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}:=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}\setminus[\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{t}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt}(\partial=1)].

Since the RHS of (
5.56
) must be equal to the LHS of (
5.61
),
we deduce that

\mathcal{Y}=\{W(q_{i})\ |\ i=1,\ldots,l\}

and that equality holds in (
5.57
) for each
i=1,\ldots,l
.
Since the third sum in the RHS of (
5.61
) vanishes,
we conclude that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}(W(q_{i}))|=2
for each
i=1,\ldots,l
.
Finally,
|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|=|\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}|-1

because
2\#_{c}(\partial[W\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])-3=1

for each
W\in\mathcal{Y}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
5.29
.
∎

We continue proving Theorem
5.27
. We can estimate (
5.50
)
as follows:

\displaystyle 2e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.16a}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})

(5.62)

\displaystyle\hskip-241.84842pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9.18a},\eqref{9.19a},\eqref{9.17a}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\hskip-128.0374pt|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|+2\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)\right|+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1+\sum_{W\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(\partial>1)}3I(W).

In order to bound from below the last sum in (
5.62
), note that
if
W\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(\partial>1)
, then either
W
is flat (and then

I(W)=0
), or
W
is orientable and non-flat (in which case we estimate

I(W)\geq 1
), or
W
is non-orientable with
|W\cap\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}|=1

and
\#_{c}(\partial[W\cap\Sigma^{c}])>1
(in which case we estimate

I(W)\geq 2
by item 2 of Lemma
3.4
),
or else
W
is non-orientable
with
|W\cap\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}|>1
(in which case we estimate
I(W)\geq 0
).
Therefore, calling

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{*}=\{W\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}\ |\ W\mbox{ is non-orientable, }|W\cap\mathcal{S}_{\rm T}|=1,\ \#_{c}(\partial[W\cap\Sigma_{\rm T}^{c}])>1\},\\
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)=\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)\cap\mathcal{W}_{\rm T},\end{array}

we deduce that

\sum_{W\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}(\partial>1)}3I(W)\geq 6|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{*}|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|.

(5.63)

Using that
|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{*}|\geq 0
, from (
5.62
)
and (
5.63
) we get the following estimate from below
for (
5.50
):

2e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})\geq(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1)+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|+2\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)\right|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|.

(5.64)

By the additivity in components of the correction term
C(\mathcal{H})
defined
in (
5.42
), we can write
C(\mathcal{H})
as the sum of

C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})

plus the terms in (
5.42
) that are added in the top level,
that is,

C(\mathcal{H})=C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})+\left[3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m})+(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1)+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|+2\left|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)\right|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|\right].

(5.65)

Thus, (
5.64
) and (
5.65
) give

2e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})\geq C(\mathcal{H})-C(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{nm})-3I^{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T}^{m}).

(5.66)

By (
5.66
), the sum of (
5.50
) and (
5.51
)
is at least
C(\mathcal{H})
. Adding this last inequality with (
5.49
)
we obtain (
5.41
), as desired. This completes the
proof of Theorem
5.27
.
∎

Definition 5.30
.

Observe that given
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, the compact piece
\Delta_{q}

has itself a hierarchy
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{q},\mathcal{V}_{q},\mathcal{W}_{q})
,
whose related sets are subsets of the corresponding ones for
the hierarchy of
\Delta
, i.e.,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{q}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
,

\mathcal{V}_{q}\subset\mathcal{V}
, and

\mathcal{W}_{q}\subset\mathcal{W}
.
Clearly, the hierarchy of
\Delta_{q}
has strictly less levels than the
hierarchy of
\Delta
.
We define
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
to be the number of
levels in
\mathcal{H}
which consist of one
\Delta_{q}
piece (equivalently, the
number of levels in
\mathcal{H}
whose singular set is unitary) if
\mathcal{H}
is
non-trivial. If
\mathcal{H}
is trivial, we let
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})=0
.

For instance, the hierarchy given in item 2 of Example
5.24

has
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})=1
, and the one in item 3 of
Example
5.24

(given by Figure
5
) has
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})=2
.

Corollary 5.31
.

Let
\Delta,\mathcal{H}
be as in Theorem
5.27
, with
\mathcal{H}
non-trivial.
If
\Delta
is non-orientable, then
inequality (
5.41
) holds after replacing
C(\mathcal{H})
by the following
correction term:

C^{no}(\mathcal{H})=:C(\mathcal{H})+6|\mathcal{W}^{*}|\geq 3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|-L+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})\geq L,

(5.67)

where
\mathcal{W}^{*}
is the set of components
W\in\mathcal{W}
which are non-orientable
with
|W\cap\mathcal{S}|=1
and
\#_{c}(\partial(W\setminus\mathcal{V}^{c}))>1
; here

\mathcal{V}^{c}=\cup_{q\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{D}_{q}
and
\mathcal{D}_{q}
was defined just
before (
5.34
).

Proof.

In passing from (
5.63
) to (
5.64
) in the derivation of
the correction term
C(\mathcal{H})
of (
5.41
), we neglected to keep the
term
6|\mathcal{W}^{*}_{\rm T}|
of (
5.63
). If we include this term
(which can only be non-zero provided that
\Delta
is
non-orientable), then previous
calculations in the derivation of
C(\mathcal{H})
imply that
inequality (
5.41
) holds after replacing
C(\mathcal{H})
by

C(\mathcal{H})+6|\mathcal{W}^{*}|
.

Next we prove both inequalities in (
5.67
). Both inequalities are
additive in the levels of the hierarchy, so it suffices to prove that
each level
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}
satisfies

C^{no}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 3I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 1,

(5.68)

where
C^{no}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
,
I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
,

|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|
,
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
denote the
related numbers referred just to the level
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, for instance

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\neq\varnothing
is the singular set of
the level
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
C^{no}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
is given by

C^{no}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1+|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,f}|+2|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|+6|\mathcal{W}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|,

(5.69)

and
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
takes the value
1
if

|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|=1

and
0
if
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|\geq 2
.

We will prove that (
5.68
) holds by considering two mutually
exclusive cases.

(a)

Suppose that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|\geq 2
.
In this case, the second inequality in (
5.68
) clearly holds. Since

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=0
, then the first inequality also
holds.

(b)

Suppose now that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|=1
. Thus,

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=1
and at least one of the terms

|\mathcal{W}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|
,
|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|
,

|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|

is positive, which proves that the first inequality
in (
5.68
) holds. The second inequality also holds since

3I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=2.

Hence, (
5.68
) holds and the corollary is proved.
∎

In order to state and prove the orientable version of
Corollary
5.31
we will
need the following lemma (compare to Lemma
5.29
).

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
\Delta,\mathcal{H}
be as in Theorem
5.27
, with
\mathcal{H}
non-trivial. If
\Delta
is orientable, then,

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|\geq 2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1).

(5.70)

Let
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
be as defined in Lemma
5.29
. Then:

1.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|=1
and equality in (
5.70
) holds, then

\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)=\varnothing
(equivalently,
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,t}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt}(\partial=1)
).

2.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|>1
and equality occurs in (
5.70
),
then
\mathcal{Y}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
,
W^{c}
has exactly
two boundary components for each
W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}
,
|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1
and every component in
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
is flat.

Proof.

If
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|=1
, then (
5.70
) clearly holds as well as
item 1, by the same reason as in the proof of Lemma
5.29
.
Assume that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|>1
. Since

\mathcal{Y}^{c}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
,

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)}\hskip-14.22636pt(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}|\geq\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}\cap\mathcal{Y}^{c}|

(5.71)

with equality if and only if
\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{c}=\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c}(\partial>1)
.

Suppose that
W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}
has
l\geq 2
boundary curves. If
W^{c}
is
non-flat, then it makes a contribution of at least
2l
to the RHS of
(
5.71
) (note that
I(W^{c})\geq 1
because
W^{c}
is orientable and
non-flat); on the other hand, if
W^{c}
is flat then it makes
a contribution of at least
2l-2
to the RHS of (
5.71
). Thus,
the RHS of (
5.71
) takes on its smallest possible value precisely
when every component of
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
is flat; in this case, we get the next
lower estimate for the RHS of (
5.71
) with equality if and only if
every component of
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
is flat:

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3+3I(W^{c}))+|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{c,nt,f}\cap\mathcal{Y}^{c}|\geq\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3)+\left|\mathcal{Y}^{c}\right|.

(5.72)

Finally, a calculation similar to the one used to prove
Lemma
5.29
demonstrates that the minimum value of the RHS
of (
5.72
) occurs precisely when

\mathcal{Y}^{c}
satisfies the second statement in Lemma
5.29
; in
particular,
|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1
in this case.
Applying (
5.55
), we have

\sum_{W^{c}\in\mathcal{Y}^{c}}(2\#_{c}(\partial W^{c})-3)+\left|\mathcal{Y}^{c}\right|\geq\left|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}\right|-1+|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|,

(5.73)

with equality if and only if
|\mathcal{Y}^{c}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1
by item 2 of
Lemma
5.29
, in which case the RHS of (
5.73
) equals

2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1)
. This completes the proof of (
5.70
).
Item 2 of Lemma
5.32
concerning
\mathcal{Y}^{c}
follows as well from
the above discussion. Now the proof of Lemma
5.32
is finished.
∎

Corollary 5.33
.

Let
\Delta,\mathcal{H}
be as in Theorem
5.27
. If
\Delta
is
orientable, then inequality (
5.41
) holds after replacing

C(\mathcal{H})
by the following correction term:

C^{or}(\mathcal{H})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})+2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|-L)+2|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+3|\mathcal{W}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|.

(5.74)

Furthermore, the new correction term satisfies

C^{or}(\mathcal{H})\geq 3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})+2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|-L)+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})\geq 2L.

(5.75)

Proof.

The argument is very similar to the one for proving
Corollary
5.31
, so
we will only focus on the differences and use the same notation.
We first check that

6\,I(\Delta)\geq-\chi(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)+C^{or}(\mathcal{H}),

(5.76)

where
C^{or}(\mathcal{H})
is given by equation (
5.74
).
The proof of this fact proceeds exactly as in the proof of (
5.41
)
for the correction term
C(\mathcal{H})
, except in the estimate in (
5.64
)
one uses Lemma
5.32
to obtain

2e(\mathcal{V}_{\rm T})-3\#_{c}(\Sigma_{\rm T})+3I(\Sigma_{\rm T})+\#_{c}(\Sigma^{f}_{\rm T})\geq 2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm T}|-1)+2|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\rm T}^{nt,nf,or}(\partial>1)|.

This completes the proof that for
\Delta
orientable, (
5.76
)
holds.

We next prove (
5.75
) holds. If
\mathcal{H}
is trivial, then
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|=L=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})=0
and

C^{or}(\mathcal{H})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{I*}}}{{=}}3I(\Delta)-3.

Consequently, equality holds in the first inequality
of (
5.75
), while the second inequality reduces to
3I(\Delta)-3\geq 0
, which holds since
I(\Delta)\geq 1
.
Suppose in the sequel that
\mathcal{H}
is non-trivial.
By additivity, we can reduce the proof to the case that each level
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}

satisfies

C^{or}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 3I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})+2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1)+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 2,

(5.77)

where

C^{or}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=3I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})+2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1)+2|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+3|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1)|.

First suppose that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|\geq 2
.
In this case, the second inequality in (
5.77
) clearly holds. Since

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=0
in this case, then the first inequality
in (
5.77
) also holds.

Suppose now that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|=1
, and so

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=1
. The second
inequality in (
5.77
) holds because
I^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq 0
and

2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})|-1)+2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=2
.
The first inequality also holds because in this case,

2|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial=1)|+2|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{nt,nf}(\partial>1)|\geq 2=2\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
.
Hence, (
5.77
) holds and the corollary is proved.
∎

Proposition 5.34
.

Let
\Delta,\mathcal{H}
be as in Theorem
5.27
, with
\Delta
connected.

1.

If
I(\Delta)=1
, then
\mathcal{H}
is trivial,
\Delta
is
orientable,
g(\Delta)=0
, and
(e(\Delta),S(\Delta))\in\{(2,2),(1,3)\}
.
In particular, equality in (
5.41
) holds.

2.

If
\mathcal{H}
is trivial and
\Delta
is orientable, then

2g(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-3
,
2e(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)+1
and

2S(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)+3
.

3.

If
\mathcal{H}
is trivial and
\Delta
is non-orientable, then

I(\Delta)\geq 2
,
S(\Delta)\geq 3
,

g(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-4
,
2e(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-2

and
2S(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)+2
.

4.

If
\mathcal{H}
is non-trivial with
L>0
levels, then
S(\Delta)\geq 2
and

I(\Delta)\geq 1+L
.

5.

If
\mathcal{H}
is non-trivial with
L>0
levels
and
\Delta
is orientable, then

g(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-L-3
,
e(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-L-1

and
S(\Delta)\leq 3I(\Delta)-L
.

6.

If
\mathcal{H}
is non-trivial with
L>0
levels and
\Delta
is
non-orientable, then
g(\Delta)\leq 6I(\Delta)-L-7
,

2e\leq 6I-L-3
and
2S(\Delta)\leq 6I(\Delta)-L-1
.

Proof.

Suppose
I(\Delta)=1
. Then, the non-flat limit minimal immersion

f_{1}\colon\Sigma_{1}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
found in Section
5.5.1
has index 1,
and Proposition
5.3
implies that the hierarchy
\mathcal{H}
of

\Delta
is trivial. Furthermore, Theorem 1.8 in
[
6
]
ensures
that
f_{1}
must be two-sided, and since the index of
f_{1}
is one,

f_{1}(\Sigma_{1})
is either a catenoid or an Enneper minimal
surface
[
14
]
. In particular,
g(\Delta)=0
and

(e(\Delta),S(\Delta))\in\{(2,2),(1,3)\}
. This proves
item
1
.

To prove items
2
and
3
,
suppose that
\mathcal{H}
is trivial.
By Theorem
5.27
, (
5.41
) can be
written as
3I(\Delta)\geq-\chi(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)-3
.
After replacing
\chi(\Delta)
by
2-2g(\Delta)-e(\Delta)

provided that
\Delta
is orientable (resp. by
1-g(\Delta)-e(\Delta)
if

\Delta
is non-orientable), we get

\begin{array}[]{ccrl}3I(\Delta)&\geq&2g(\Delta)+2e(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)-5&\mbox{if $\Delta$ is orientable,}\\
3I(\Delta)&\geq&g(\Delta)+2e(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)-4&\mbox{if $\Delta$ is non-orientable.}\end{array}

We next discuss on the orientability character of
\Delta
.
If
\Delta
is orientable, the estimates from above for each of

g(\Delta),e(\Delta),S(\Delta)
in
item
2
of the proposition follow from a straightforward
computation using two of the inequalities
g(\Delta)\geq 0
,

e(\Delta)\geq 1
,
S(\Delta)\geq 2
,
e(\Delta)+S(\Delta)\geq 4
.
If
\Delta
is non-orientable (in particular,
I(\Delta)\geq 2

by item 1 of this
proposition) and we additionally suppose that
S(\Delta)=2
, then the area
growth at infinity of
f_{1}
is that of two planes, which prevents

f_{1}
from having self-intersections by the monotonicity formula for area;
therefore,
f_{1}
is properly embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which contradicts
that
\Delta
is non-orientable. Therefore,
S(\Delta)\geq 3
provided
that
\Delta
is non-orientable. Now similar arguments as those
in the orientable case show that the upper estimates for

g(\Delta),e(\Delta),S(\Delta)
in item
3
of the proposition
hold.

Next suppose that
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is non-trivial with
L>0
levels.
This implies that we can find
L+1
blow-up limits

f_{i}\colon\Sigma_{i}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad i=1,\ldots,L+1

of suitable rescalings
\{{\lambda}_{i,n}F_{n}\}_{n}
of the original sequence

\{F_{n}\}_{n}
as in item (S2) above (centered at possibly different
points where the second
fundamental form of
F_{n}
blows-up). Since the index increases each time
we add a level (by item 8(f) of Proposition
5.13
), then we
deduce that
I(\Delta)\geq L+1
. Since the total spinning of
f_{1}

is at least two, the monotonicity formula implies that
S(\Delta)\geq 2
.
This completes the proof of item
4
.

We finish by proving item
5
and
6
, so continue
assuming that
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
is non-trivial with
L>0
levels,
and suppose
\Delta
is connected.
In the case that
\Delta
is orientable, we apply
Corollary
5.33
with the
estimate for the correction term
C^{or}(\mathcal{H})
given
in (
5.75
), obtaining

\displaystyle 3\,I(\Delta)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textstyle{-\frac{1}{2}\chi(\Delta)+S(\Delta)+\frac{1}{2}e(\Delta)+L}

(5.78)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle g(\Delta)+S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)-1+L,

where in
(\star)
we have used that

\chi(\Delta)=2-2g(\Delta)-e(\Delta)
.

In the case
\Delta
is non-orientable, we apply
Corollary
5.31
with the
estimate for the correction term
C^{no}(\mathcal{H})

given in (
5.67
),
obtaining

\displaystyle 6\,I(\Delta)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\chi(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+e(\Delta)+L

(5.79)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)^{\prime}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle g(\Delta)+2S(\Delta)+2e(\Delta)-1+L,

where in
(\star)^{\prime}
we have used that

\chi(\Delta)=1-g(\Delta)-e(\Delta)
.

With inequalities (
5.78
), (
5.79
) at hand, each of the
estimates from above for of
g(\Delta),e(\Delta),S(\Delta)
in
items
5
and
6
of the proposition
follow from a straightforward computation using
two of the inequalities

g(\Delta)\geq 0
,
e(\Delta)\geq 1
,
S(\Delta)\geq 2
(which holds
by item
4
) and
e(\Delta)+S(\Delta)\geq 4
.
This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

5.7
Proofs of items (
A
)-…-(
D
)
of Theorem
1.2

Next we will focus on the second step in our strategy of proving
Theorem
1.2
, see Section
5.3
.

Item
A
of Theorem
1.2
follows from the fact that

\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}
are pairwise disjoint (by the already
proven item 1(c) of Theorem
1.2
).

We next prove item
B
. The inequality
2\leq m=S(\Delta)

for the total spinning of the boundary of
\Delta=\Delta_{i}
follows since
each local picture of any element
F\in\Lambda
has at least
either two embedded ends, or one immersed end of Enneper type,
with spinning number at least
3
; also see item
4
of
Proposition
5.34
. Item
B
(a)
was proven in item
1
of Proposition
5.34
.

Now assume
\Delta
is orientable and
I(\Delta)\geq 2
. Then,
items
2
and
5
of Proposition
5.34

give that

\displaystyle S(\Delta)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\{\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)+3)},3I(\Delta)-L\}\leq\max\{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)+3),3I(\Delta)-1\}=3I(\Delta)-1,

\displaystyle e(\Delta)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\{\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)+1)},3I(\Delta)-L-1\}\leq\max\{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)+1),3I(\Delta)-2\}=3I(\Delta)-2,

\displaystyle g(\Delta)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\{\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)-3)},3I(\Delta)-L-3\}\leq\max\{\frac{1}{2}(3I(\Delta)-3),3I(\Delta)-4\}=3I(\Delta)-4,

where
L\geq 1
is the number of levels of the hierarchy of
\Delta

provided this hierarchy is non-trivial. This proves item
B
(b)
of Theorem
1.2
. Item
B
(c) can be proven in
the same way, using items
3
and
6
of
Proposition
5.34
(the fact that
I(\Delta)\geq 2
follows from
items
3
and
4
of Proposition
5.34
),
we leave the details to the reader.

The inequality
\chi(\Delta_{i})\geq-6I(\Delta_{i})+2m(i)+e(i)

in item B(d) of Theorem
1.2
follows directly
from (
5.41
): observe that the multiplicity of the multi-graph
associated to each boundary component (resp. the number of boundary
components) of
\Delta=\Delta_{i}
is
m(i)
(resp.
e(i)
)
with the notation of Theorem
1.2
.

The inequality
|\kappa(\Delta_{i})-2\pi m(i)|\leq\frac{\tau}{m(i)}
in
Item B(e) of Theorem
1.2
follows from the multi-graphical
structure proven in item 2 of Theorem
1.2
and from
Lemma
4.4
. As for the second inequality in Item B(e) of
Theorem
1.2
,

\left|\kappa(\widetilde{M})+2\pi S\right|=\left|-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\kappa(\Delta_{i})+2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}m(i)\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|\kappa(\Delta_{i})-2\pi m(i)\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\tau}{m(i)}\leq\frac{\tau}{2}k.

Equation (
1.2
) follows directly from the last inequality, since

\kappa(\widetilde{M})=-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\kappa(\Delta_{i})
.

To finish the proof of item
B
of Theorem
1.2
,
it remains to demonstrate item
B
(f), which we do next.
Choose a minimal element
\Delta_{q}
in the hierarchy

\mathcal{H}(\Delta)=(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})

of
\mathcal{H}
, being
q\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
. Then,
\Delta_{q}=\Delta_{q}(n)
is a
connected compact surface with boundary inside
M_{n}
, and for
n
large
enough, a certain rescaling of
\Delta_{q}(n)
resembles arbitrarily well
the intersection with a large ball of a connected, complete, non-flat
minimal immersion
f\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
with finite total
curvature (see property (S1) above). As the total curvature of this limit
immersion
f
is a negative multiple of
4\pi
when
\Sigma
is orientable
and it is at least
-2\pi
if
\Sigma
is non-orientable with the value

-2\pi
implying that
f
is stable (see item 1 of the discussion in
Section 3 of
[
17
]
), and the total curvature is invariant under
rescaling, we deduce that

-\int_{\Delta_{q}(n)}K>3\pi

for
n
large enough.
When we ascend one level in
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
of
\mathcal{H}
passing from

\Delta_{q}
to some
\Delta_{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{V}
with
q^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and

\Delta_{q}\preceq\Delta_{q^{\prime}}
, then a similar description holds
for
\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)
with
n
large, with the difference that the related
complete minimal surface
f^{\prime}\colon\Sigma^{\prime}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
with finite total
curvature associated to
\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)
may be flat, finitely
disconnected and finitely branched,
and the convergence of suitably rescaled portions of
\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)
to
a compact portion of
f^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime})
is away from finitely many points of

f(\Sigma^{\prime})
, of which at least one corresponds to
f^{\prime}(q)
. Since

-\int_{\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)}K=-\int_{\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)\setminus\Delta_{q}(n)}K-\int_{\Delta_{q}(n)}K

and the first integral is either close to zero or larger than
3\pi
for

n
large, we deduce that
-\int_{\Delta_{q^{\prime}}(n)}K>3\pi

for
n
sufficiently large. Iterating
this process finitely many times, we get that
-\int_{\Delta}K>3\pi
,
as desired.
Adding up this last inequality in
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}

and using the Gauss-Bonnet formula, we deduce that
inequality (
1.3
) holds. Now the proof of
item
B
of Theorem
1.2
is complete.

We next prove item
C
of Theorem
1.2
.
Suppose that the genus
g(M)
of
M
is finite and that
k\geq 1
.

Elementary surface topology of orientable surfaces implies that if
\Sigma

is a possibly disconnected orientable
surface (possibly with boundary) and
\Delta
is a compact, possibly
disconnected, smooth subsurface in the interior of
\Sigma
,
then the genus
g(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
, the genus
g(\Delta)
of
\Delta
and the
genus
g(\widetilde{\Sigma})
of
\widetilde{\Sigma}=\Sigma\setminus\Delta
satisfy the following
inequality:

g(\Sigma)\leq g(\widetilde{\Sigma})+g(\Delta)+\#_{c}(\partial\Delta)-\#_{c}(\Delta),

(5.80)

with equality if and only if each component of
\Delta
does not
disconnect the component of
\Sigma
that contains it.

Applying (
5.80
) to
M
with
\Delta=\cup_{i=1}^{k}\Delta_{i}
, gives

g(M)\leq g(\widetilde{M})+g(\cup_{i=1}^{k}\Delta_{i})+\#_{c}(\cup_{i=1}^{k}\partial\Delta_{i})-k.

(5.81)

Hence,

g(M)-g(\widetilde{M})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}[g(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-1].

(5.82)

If a domain
\Delta_{i}
has
trivial hierarchy, then (
5.41
) reduces to (
3.5
) and
thus,

3I(\Delta_{i})\geq-\chi(\Delta_{i})+2S(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-3\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}2g(\Delta_{i})+2e(\Delta_{i})+2S(\Delta_{i})-5,

where in
(\star)
we have used that

\chi(\Delta_{i})=2-2g(\Delta_{i})-e(\Delta_{i})
as
\Delta_{i}
must be
orientable. Therefore, in this case,

g(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-1\leq\textstyle{\frac{3}{2}I(\Delta_{i})-S(\Delta_{i})+\frac{3}{2}}.

(5.83)

If
\Delta_{i}
has non-trivial hierarchy with
L_{i}\geq 1
levels,
then (
5.76
) and (
5.75
) imply

6I(\Delta_{i})\geq-\chi(\Delta_{i})+2S(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})+2L_{i}=2g(\Delta_{i})+2e(\Delta_{i})+2S(\Delta_{i})+2L_{i}-2.

Thus, in this case,

g(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-1\leq 3I(\Delta_{i})-S(\Delta_{i})-L_{i}\leq 3I(\Delta_{i})-S(\Delta_{i})-1.

(5.84)

Now (
5.83
) and (
5.84
) give the common upper bound estimate

g(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-1\leq\max\left\{\textstyle{\frac{3}{2}I(\Delta_{i})+\frac{3}{2}},3I(\Delta_{i})-1\right\}-S(\Delta_{i}).

(5.85)

The function
\max\left\{\textstyle{\frac{3}{2}I(\Delta_{i})+\frac{3}{2}},3I(\Delta_{i})-1\right\}
has the value 3 if
I(\Delta_{i})=1
and the value

3I(\Delta_{i})-1
if
I(\Delta_{i})\geq 2
;
hence,

\max\left\{\textstyle{\frac{3}{2}I(\Delta_{i})+\frac{3}{2}},3I(\Delta_{i})-1\right\}\leq 3I(\Delta_{i})
in all cases.
Therefore, since it also holds that
S(\Delta_{i})\geq 2

for
all
i
, then (
5.85
) gives

g(\Delta_{i})+e(\Delta_{i})-1\leq 3I(\Delta_{i})-S(\Delta_{i})\leq 3I(\Delta_{i})-2\qquad\mbox{for all $i=1,\ldots,k$.}

(5.86)

From (
5.82
) and (
5.86
) we deduce that

g(M)-g(\widetilde{M})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}(3I(\Delta_{i})-2)\leq 3I-2k\leq 3I-2

(5.87)

which gives the desired inequality in item
C
of
Theorem
1.2
.

To finish the proof of Theorem
1.2
, it remains to
demonstrate item
D
, which we do next. Suppose
k\geq 1
.
Item
D
will be proven in three steps.

(R1)

\mbox{\rm Area}(\Delta_{i})\leq 2\pi m(i)r_{F}(i)^{2}
provided that
the constant
A_{1}\in[A_{0},\infty)
given in the main statement of
Theorem
1.2
is sufficiently large.

We will assume
i=1
in order to use the notation introduced in
Section
5.5
; the cases
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
are similar.

Recall from property (P1) above (and with the notation there)
that the intersection of

F(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1})
between the extrinsic spheres
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})
and
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4})

consists of
e_{s_{0}}
multi-graphical annuli
\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1),\ldots,\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(e_{s_{0}})
.
Also recall (first paragraph
after property (K2)’) that
\Delta_{1}
was defined as the component of

F^{-1}(\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),r_{F}(1))
that contains
p_{1}
, where

r_{F}(1)={\delta}_{1}={\delta}_{4}/4
and
{\delta}_{4}
is given by
Proposition
5.16
.

For
j=1,\ldots,e_{s_{0}}
, define

\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(j,R_{s_{0}}/t,r_{F}(1))

to be portion of
F(\Delta_{1})\cap\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(j)
between
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),R_{s_{0}}/t)
and
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{1}),r_{F}(1))
. Thus,

\bigcup_{j=1}^{e_{s_{0}}}\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(j,R_{s_{0}}/t,r_{F}(1))=F(\Delta_{1})\setminus\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),R_{s_{0}}/t).

Therefore,

\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\Delta_{1})}{\pi m(1)r_{F}(1)^{2}}=\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}[\Delta_{1}\cap F^{-1}(\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),R_{s_{0}}/t))]}{\pi m(1)r_{F}(1)^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{e_{s_{0}}}\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(j,R_{s_{0}}/t,r_{F}(1))}{\pi m(1)r_{F}(1)^{2}}.

(5.88)

Observe that for
t
sufficiently large (equivalently, for
A_{1}

sufficiently large, see equation (
5.30
)),
the extrinsic radius

R_{s_{0}}/t
becomes arbitrarily small (because
R_{s_{0}}

is independent of
t
) and so, the first term of the
RHS of (
5.88
) also becomes arbitrarily small
for
A_{1}
sufficiently large. Regarding the second term of the
RHS of (
5.88
), observe that

\sum_{j=1}^{e_{s_{0}}}\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(j,R_{s_{0}}/t,r_{F}(1))}{\pi m(1)r_{F}(1)^{2}}\approx\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}[f_{s_{0}}(\Sigma_{s_{0}})\cap\mathbb{B}(\vec{0},t\,r_{F}(1)]}{m(1)\,\mbox{\rm Area}(\mathbb{D}(\vec{0},t\,r_{F}(1)))},

(5.89)

where
f_{s_{0}}\colon\Sigma_{s_{0}}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the complete, finitely branched
minimal immersion with finite total curvature
defined in the paragraph just before Proposition
5.16

(with the notation there,
{\lambda}_{s_{0},n}=\frac{1}{r_{s_{0},n}}

plays the role of
t
in our current notation),
and the symbol
\approx
means arbitrarily close for
t

large (to check this, re-scale the ambient metric of
X
around

F(p_{1})
by the factor
t
and use either property (S2.a) or else
the adaptation of Proposition
5.13
after replacing
f_{2}

by
f_{s_{0}}
).
Now, the monotonicity formula for minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
implies that
the quotient in (
5.89
) is less than or equal to 1
(and arbitrarily close to 1 provided that
t
is large enough).
Therefore, (
5.88
) ensures that if
t
is sufficiently large, we
have

\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\Delta_{1})}{\pi m(1)r_{F}(1)^{2}}\leq 2,

which proves property (R1).

(R2)

\pi{\delta}_{1}^{2}\leq\mbox{\rm Area}(\Delta_{i})
provided that

A_{1}
is sufficiently large.

Using the notation of the already proven item
2

of Theorem
1.2
, is clearly suffices to prove that

\mbox{\rm Area}\left(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}G_{i,h}\right)\geq\pi{\delta}_{1}^{2}

provided that
A_{1}
is sufficiently large. Recall from
item
2
of Theorem
1.2

that
G_{i,h}
is an annular
multi-graph (of multiplicity
m_{i,h}
) over its projection

\Omega_{i,h}

to
P_{i,h}=\varphi_{F(p_{i})}(\mathbb{D}_{h})
and the boundary of

G_{i,h}
consists of two curves, each one lying on one of the extrinsic
spheres
\partial B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i)/2)
and

\partial B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i))
. Observe that the quotient

\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}G_{i,h})}{\mbox{\rm Area}(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}\Omega_{i,h})}

is invariant under re-scaling of the ambient metric centered at
F(p_{i})
.
Arguing similarly as in (
5.89
) and with the notation there, we
have that for
t
sufficiently large

\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}G_{i,h})}{\mbox{\rm Area}(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}\Omega_{i,h})}\approx\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}[f_{s_{0}}(\Sigma_{s_{0}})\cap(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},t\,r_{F}(i))\setminus\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},t\,r_{F}(i)/2)]}{e(i)\,\pi t^{2}[r_{F}(i)^{2}-r_{F}(i)^{2}/4]}\approx m(i).

Therefore,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mbox{\rm Area}\left(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}G_{i,h}\right)&\approx&m(i)\,\mbox{\rm Area}(\cup_{h=1}^{e(i)}\Omega_{i,h})\\
&\approx&m(i)\pi[r_{F}(i)^{2}-\textstyle{\frac{r_{F}(i)^{2}}{4}}]=m(i)\frac{3\pi}{4}r_{F}(i)^{2}\geq m(i)\frac{3\pi}{4}{\delta}_{1}^{2}\geq\pi{\delta}_{1}^{2},\end{array}

where in the last equality we have used that
m(i)\geq 2
.

(R3)

\mbox{\rm Area}(\widetilde{M})\geq 14\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}m(i)r_{F}(i)^{2}
.

We continue using the notation of item (R1).
Recall that for
t
sufficiently large,

F(M)
contains
e_{s_{0}}
annular multi-graphs

\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1),\dots,\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(e_{s_{0}})
in

\overline{B}_{X}(F(p_{1}),{\delta}_{4})\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),\frac{R_{s_{0}}}{2t})
and

e_{s_{0}}
is the number of ends of
f_{s_{0}}
, and
[\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1)\cup\dots\cup\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(e_{s_{0}})]\cap B_{X}(F(p_{1}),r_{F}(1))
is contained in
\Delta_{1}
.
Observe that the disjoint union

[\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1)\cup\ldots\cup\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(e_{s_{0}})]\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),r_{F}(1))

is contained in
\widetilde{M}
.
A similar situation holds around each of the relative
maxima
p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
of
|A_{M}|

(in the sense of item
1
(d) of Theorem
1.2
), which
produces corresponding annular multi-graphs inside

\widetilde{M}
that will be denoted by

[\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1,1)\cup\dots\cup\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(1,e_{s_{0,1}})]\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{1}),r_{F}(1))

‘around’
p_{1}
,

\cdots

\cdots

[\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(k,1)\cup\dots\cup\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(k,e_{s_{0,k}})]\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{k}),r_{F}(k))

‘around’
p_{k}
,

all pairwise disjoint. Therefore,

\mbox{\rm Area}(\widetilde{M})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mbox{Area}\left[\left(\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(i,1)\cup\dots\cup\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(i,e_{s_{0,i}})\right)\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i))\right]

(5.90)

Given
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
h\in\{1,\ldots,e_{s_{0},i}\}
,
we call
\Omega^{\prime}_{i,h}
to the projection of
\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(i,h)\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i))
to the corresponding ‘disk’
P_{i,h}
defined as in
item
2
of Theorem
1.2
.
Arguing as in item (R2), we have

\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}\left[(\cup_{h=1}^{e_{s_{0,i}}}\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(i,h))\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i))\right]}{\mbox{\rm Area}\left(\cup_{h=1}^{e_{s_{0,i}}}\Omega^{\prime}_{i,h}\right)}\approx\frac{\mbox{\rm Area}[f_{s_{0,i}}(\Sigma_{s_{0,i}})\cap(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},t\,{\delta}_{4})\setminus\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\vec{0},t\,r_{F}(i))]}{e_{s_{0},i}\,\pi t^{2}[{\delta}_{4}^{2}-r_{F}(i)^{2}]}\approx m(i),

where
f_{s_{0,i}}\colon\Sigma_{s_{0,i}}\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}

is the corresponding complete, finitely branched minimal surface of
finite total curvature obtained as a local picture
around
F(p_{i})
, and
e_{s_{0,i}}
is the number of its ends.

Therefore,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mbox{\rm Area}\left[(\cup_{h=1}^{e_{s_{0,i}}}\widehat{G}_{s_{0}}(i,h))\setminus B_{X}(F(p_{i}),r_{F}(i))\right]&\approx&m(i)\,\mbox{\rm Area}\left(\cup_{h=1}^{e_{s_{0,i}}}\Omega^{\prime}_{i,h}\right)\\
&\approx&m(i)\pi[{\delta}_{4}^{2}-r_{F}(i)^{2}]=15\,m(i)\pi r_{F}(i)^{2}.\end{array}

From here and (
5.90
) one concludes directly
the inequality (R3), which completes the proof of item
D

of Theorem
1.2
.

6
Sequential compactness results in
\Lambda
for
X
fixed

Fix
I\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. An important consequence of the
statement and proof of the Structure
Theorem
1.2
is that
certain sequences of immersions in
\Lambda=\Lambda(I,H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})

have natural limits
that are finitely branched
H
-surfaces for some
H\in[0,H_{0}]
.
A special case of this behavior applies to the following situation.
Suppose that
\{F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright X\}_{n}

is a sequence in
\Lambda
with common ambient space
X
,
the
M_{n}
are connected with empty boundary,
and the norm of the second fundamental forms of
F_{n}

are sufficiently large so that the points
p_{1}(n)\in M_{n}
defined in
Theorem
1.2
exist and the sequence of points

F_{n}(p_{1}(n))=x_{n}
converges to
x\in X
. If in addition the norms of
the second fundamental forms of the
F_{n}
are uniformly bounded,
then a subsequence of the
F_{n}
converges smoothly on compact balls
in
M_{n}
centered at
p_{1}(n)
to a complete immersed
surface
F_{\infty}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright X\in\Lambda

of constant mean curvature with a special point

p_{1}(\infty)\in\Sigma
with
F_{\infty}(p_{1}(\infty))=x
.
The next theorem proves a similar result
holds when the norms of the second fundamental forms of the
F_{n}
at

p_{1}(n)
diverge to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
However, while the complete limit mapping
F_{\infty}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright X

in this case is smooth and defined on a limit
Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the convergence is not smooth at a non-empty finite
set
\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma}\subset\Sigma
of points and
F_{\infty}
may have
a finite set of branch points that form a subset of
\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma}
, where the
total branching order is at most
3I
and the index of
F_{\infty}
is at
most
I-1
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Given
I\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\tau\in(0,\pi/10]
,
let
\Lambda=\Lambda(I,H_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0},A_{0},K_{0})
be as given in Definition
1.1
.
Let
F_{n}\colon M_{n}\looparrowright X
be a sequence of
H_{n}
-immersions
in
\Lambda
with
M_{n}
connected with empty boundary, and with
the supremum of the norms of their
second fundamental forms
|A_{F_{n}}|
greater than the constant
A_{1}=A_{1}(\Lambda)
given in Theorem
1.2
and let

\mathcal{P}_{Fn}=\{p_{1}(n),\ldots,p_{k(n)}(n)\}
be the associated
non-empty set of (distinct) points given in the
statement of the same theorem,
k(n)\leq I
.
Without loss of generality and after passing to a subsequence, we can
assume that both
k(n)=k
and
\mbox{\rm Index}(F_{n})=I^{\prime}\leq I

do not depend on
n
, and that
\lim_{n\to\infty}H_{n}=H_{\infty}\in[0,H_{0}]
.

Suppose that the points
F_{n}(p_{1}(n))
converge as
n\to\infty
to
a point
x_{1}\in X
and the norms of the second fundamental forms of
F_{n}

at the points
p_{1}(n)
are unbounded. Let
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
be the
cardinality of the set of points in
\mathcal{P}_{F_{n}}
which do not diverge
intrinsically from
p_{1}(n)
, i.e. after replacing by a
further subsequence and possibly re-indexing,

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{M_{n}}(p_{1}(n),p_{j}(n))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}d_{j}\in[\frac{14}{5}{\delta}_{1},\infty)&\mbox{if $2\leq j\leq k^{\prime}$,}\\
\infty&\mbox{if $k^{\prime}+1\leq j\leq k$,}\end{array}\right.

where
{\delta}_{1}>0
is defined in Theorem
1.2

and
d_{2}\leq\ldots\leq d_{k^{\prime}}
.
For each

i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
, let
\Delta_{i}(n)\subset M_{n}

be the compact subdomain described in item
1
of
Theorem
1.2
that contains the point
p_{i}(n)
.
Then, after replacing by a further subsequence:

1.

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
, the points

F_{n}(p_{i}(n))
converge
as
n\to\infty
to a point
x_{i}\in X
, where
x_{1}
is
previously defined in the hypotheses of this theorem,
and the numbers
r_{F_{n}}(p_{i})
converge to some

r_{i}\in[{\delta}_{1},{\delta}/2]
, where
{\delta}\geq 2{\delta}_{1}
is defined in
Theorem
1.2
.

2.

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
,
the
H_{n}
-multi-graphical immersions

F_{n}|_{\Delta_{i}(n)\setminus F_{n}^{-1}(B_{X}(p_{i}(n),r_{F_{n}}(i)/2))}

converge, as
n\to\infty
, to a finite collection of

e_{i}
immersed compact
H_{\infty}
-annular
multi-graphs in
\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})\setminus B_{X}(x_{i},r_{i}/2)
,
where
e_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of boundary components
of
\Delta_{i}(n)
, and the multiplicity of each of these multi-graphs
is at most
3\,\mbox{\rm Index}(\Delta_{i}(n))\leq 3I^{\prime}
;
let

F_{\infty}^{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\looparrowright\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})\setminus B_{X}(x_{i},r_{i}/2)

denote these explicit limit immersions, where

\mathcal{A}
is a finite number of compact annular Riemannian surfaces.

3.

There exists a partition
\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{U}
such that

\{|A_{F_{n}}|(p_{i}(n))\}_{n}
is bounded (resp. unbounded)
if
i\in\mathcal{B}
(resp.
i\in\mathcal{U}
); thus, we may assume
that after replacing by a further subsequence,
for each
i\in\mathcal{U}
,
|A_{F_{n}}|(p_{i}(n))>n
.

4.

For each
i\in\mathcal{B}
, the restrictions
F_{n}|_{\Delta_{i}(n)}

converge as
n\to\infty
to an
H_{\infty}
-immersion

F^{i}_{\infty}\colon\Sigma_{i}\looparrowright\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})

for some compact Riemannian surface
\Sigma_{i}
with boundary
diffeomorphic to
\Delta_{i}(n)
for
n
sufficiently large.
In this case,
F_{\infty}^{i}
has its image boundary in

\partial B_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})
and its image in

\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})\setminus B_{X}(x_{i},r_{i}/2)

consists of the
e_{i}
multi-graphs described in
item
2
.

5.

For each
i\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists a finitely
connected, finitely branched
H_{\infty}
-immersion

F_{\infty}^{i}\colon\Sigma_{i}\looparrowright\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i}),

where as in the previous case,
\Sigma_{i}
is compact with
smooth non-empty boundary and such that
we can identify
F_{\infty}^{i}
restricted to

(F_{\infty}^{i})^{-1}[\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})\setminus B_{X}(x_{i},r_{i}/2)]
with the multi-graphs in
item
2
.
Furthermore, there is a finite set

\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}\subset(F_{\infty}^{i})^{-1}[B_{X}(x_{i},r_{1}/2)]

satisfying the following properties:

(a)

The set of branch points of
F^{i}_{\infty}
is contained in

\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}
.

(b)

There exist a positive integer
J(i)\leq\mbox{\rm Index}(\Delta_{i}(n))\leq I^{\prime}
,
a finite set of points

Q_{i}(n)=\{q_{1}(i,n),\ldots,q_{J(i)}(i,n)\}\subset\mbox{\rm Int}(\Delta_{i}(n))

with
q_{1}(i,n)=p_{i}(n)
and such that for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,J(i)\}
,

|A_{F_{n}}|(q_{j}(i,n))>n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

For any
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small,
the restrictions of
F_{n}
to
\Delta_{i}(n)\setminus\bigcup_{q\in Q_{i}(n)}B_{M_{n}}(q,{\varepsilon})

converge smoothly as
n\to\infty
to

F_{\infty}^{i}
restricted to

\Sigma_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}}B_{\Sigma_{i}}(b,{\varepsilon})
, and

•

For
n
sufficiently large, the number of boundary curves
of
\bigcup_{q\in Q_{i}(n)}B_{M_{n}}(q,{\varepsilon})

coincides with the cardinality of
\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}
.

•

The restriction of
F_{\infty}^{i}
to

\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}}B_{\Sigma_{i}}(b,{\varepsilon})

is a finite collection of branched

H_{\infty}
-disks, each of which can viewed as a
multi-graph in
X
with associated finite multiplicity

S_{\infty}(b)\in\mathbb{N}
and branch point image at

F_{\infty}^{i}(b)
. Hence, the branching order
of
F^{i}_{\infty}
at a given point

b\in\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}
is equal to
S_{\infty}(b)-1
.

(d)

(Quotient space after collapsing of some points in
\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}
).
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,J(i)\}
,
there exists a non-empty subset

\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}(j)\subset\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}
which arises from the limits
of points in
\partial B_{M_{n}}(q_{j}(i,n),{\varepsilon})

as
n\to\infty
and

{\varepsilon}\to 0
. After identifying all the points in

\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}(j)
to a single point, and identifying every point of

\;\cup_{j=1}^{J(i)}(\Sigma_{i}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}(j))
with itself, we
define a quotient space
\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}
and a related quotient map

\pi_{i,j}\colon{\Sigma}_{i}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}
.
Then, the map
F_{\infty}^{i}
induces a continuous map

F^{i}_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}\to\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})
,
so that the immersions
F_{n}|_{\Delta_{i}(n)}
converge
to
F_{\infty}^{i}\colon\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}\to\overline{B}_{X}(x_{i},r_{i})
.

6.

There exists a Riemann surface
\Sigma
and a conformal
branched
H_{\infty}
-immersion
F_{\infty}\colon\Sigma\looparrowright X

satisfying the following properties:

(a)

There is a conformal embedding

f\colon\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}\to\Sigma
of the disjoint union
\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}

such that for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
,

F_{\infty}^{i}=F_{\infty}\circ(f|_{\Sigma_{i}})
, where the mappings
F_{\infty}^{i}

are defined in items
4
and
5

above. Under conformal identification via
f
, henceforth consider

\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}
to be contained in
\Sigma
.

(b)

The set of branch points of
F_{\infty}
is
contained in
\cup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}}\mathcal{B}_{\Sigma_{i}}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}
, and
so it is described in item
5
above.

(c)

F_{\infty}
can be viewed to be the limit of the immersions

F_{n}
in the following sense.
F_{\infty}
restricted to

\Sigma\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}
is the limit
in balls of
M_{n}
centered at the points
p_{1}(n)
of the
immersions
F_{n}\colon M_{n}\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{k}\Delta_{i}(n)\looparrowright X
,
and
F_{\infty}
restricted to
\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}
is the limit of
F_{n}

restricted to
\cup_{i=1}^{k}\Delta_{i}(n)
, as described
in items
4
and
5

above.

(d)

The norm of the second fundamental of
F_{\infty}
restricted
to
\Sigma\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\Sigma_{i}
is bounded by
A_{1}
,
where
A_{1}
is described in the first paragraph of the statement
of this theorem.

Proof.

Assume that Theorem
1.2
holds for
I
with associated constants

{\delta}_{1},\delta,A_{1}
. The fact that
k(n)
, Index
(F_{n})
are independent of
n

after passing to a subsequence, follows trivially since they are bounded
positive integers. Similar arguments give the convergence of
H_{n}
to
